# Jesus in Genesis — A Chapter-by-Chapter Commentary

**The Hope Connection**  
**Lewis Fuller**

*Meeting others where they are in the Name of Jesus*

*"I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and her offspring; he shall bruise your head, and you shall bruise his heel."* — Genesis 3:15 (ESV)

---

## Introduction — Genesis at a Glance

Every story has a first page, and Genesis is the first page of the only story that finally matters. The Hebrew name of the book is *Bereshit* — "In the beginning" — and that is exactly what it gives us: the beginning of the heavens and the earth, the beginning of man and woman, the beginning of sin and sorrow, the beginning of nations and languages. But above all, Genesis is the beginning of a promise. In the garden, on the worst day in human history, with the fruit still on Adam and Eve's breath and the curse ringing in the air, God spoke the first gospel the world ever heard:

> "I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and her offspring; he shall bruise your head, and you shall bruise his heel." — Genesis 3:15 (ESV)

That one sentence is the seed of everything that follows — in Genesis, and in the whole Bible after it. A child of the woman would come. He would be wounded by the serpent, bruised on the heel. And He would crush the serpent's head. Every covenant in this book, every altar, every blessing passed from father to son, every narrow escape and unlikely birth, is God keeping that first promise alive through the centuries until the Seed came. Genesis is not a book of random old stories. It is the record of a promise under attack and a God who would not let it die.

**Who wrote it.** The church has always received Genesis as the work of Moses, the first of the five books called the Pentateuch. Jesus Himself settled the question of authorship in the plainest way possible:

> "For if you believed Moses, you would believe me; for he wrote of me." — John 5:46 (ESV)

Read that again. Moses wrote *of Christ*. That is the Lord's own warrant for this whole commentary: when we open Genesis looking for Jesus, we are not inventing a reading the book cannot bear. We are reading it the way Jesus told us to read it. On the road to Emmaus, "beginning with Moses and all the Prophets, he interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning himself" (Luke 24:27). Genesis is where that walk begins.

**The shape of the book.** Genesis falls into two great halves. Chapters 1–11 are the wide-angle story of the whole world: creation, the fall, the flood, and the scattering of the nations at Babel. Four great events. Then chapters 12–50 narrow the lens to one family: Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, and Joseph. Four great men — and through that one family, God promises to bless all the families of the earth. The book keeps its own skeleton in a repeated phrase, "these are the generations of," which marks each new section like the turning of a page. And it ends, famously, not with a triumph but with "a coffin in Egypt" — a dead man's bones waiting for a promise God had not yet kept. Genesis does not finish its own story. It hands the story on, and that is the point: the God of Genesis finishes what He starts, in His time.

**Jesus in Genesis.** He is here in more ways than we can count. He is the Word by whom all things were made, and the Light that shines in the darkness of the first chapter. He is the Seed of the woman promised in the third. He appears — as the church's Reformed teachers have long read these passages, with care — in the Angel of the LORD who finds Hagar in the wilderness and receives worship from Abraham on Mount Moriah; in Melchizedek, the priest-king of Salem who brings out bread and wine and has neither beginning of days nor end of life in the record; and in the Man who wrestles Jacob until the breaking of the day and blesses him with a wounded hip and a new name. He is pictured in the ark that carries a family through the waters of judgment, in the ram caught in the thicket that dies in Isaac's place, in the ladder at Bethel with heaven open above it, and above all in Joseph — the beloved son, betrayed by his brothers, sold for silver, falsely accused, suffering in prison, then raised to the right hand of power to save the very people who sold him, and forgiving them at the end. Genesis does not give us Jesus' name. It gives us His outline, His promise, and His pattern, chapter after chapter, so that when He comes we will know Him.

**Read in the Reformation way.** This commentary reads Genesis the way the Reformers taught the church to read every book of the Bible, and their five watchwords stand at the door of this book as surely as at any other. *Sola scriptura* — Scripture alone is our rule; Genesis interprets Genesis, and every claim in these pages stands or falls by the text. *Sola gratia* — grace alone saves; nobody in this book earns God's favor, from Noah who "found favor" — grace — in the eyes of the LORD, to Jacob, who was loved before he had done anything good or bad. *Sola fide* — faith alone receives the promise; Abraham "believed the LORD, and he counted it to him as righteousness" (Genesis 15:6), the verse on which Luther's whole Reformation turned. *Solus Christus* — Christ alone is the Savior; the Seed of the woman, the Lion of Judah, the one Mediator who was promised from the first page. And *soli Deo gloria* — glory to God alone; He creates, He judges, He covenants, He provides — man receives. Along the way you will meet brief notes from Luther's great *Lectures on Genesis*, from Calvin's commentaries, and now and then from Spurgeon or Lloyd-Jones where they genuinely shed light. And in every chapter the law and the gospel are kept distinct, the way the Reformers insisted: the law shows us our sin and our need (the bad news), and the gospel shows us Christ and His finished work (the good news). In Genesis the bad news is very bad — a ruined garden, a murdered brother, a flooded world, a family that lies and steals and limps. And the good news is very good, because at every ruin stands a God who speaks first, seeks first, and provides first.

**How to use this commentary.** Each chapter section begins with "Jesus in this chapter" — a short look at how Christ is revealed there. Then the commentary walks the chapter's movements in order, explaining the text plainly and fully, with the details Scripture itself gives — the ark's dimensions, the covenant signs, the years and ages — because God put them there on purpose. Key verses are quoted in full from the English Standard Version. Each chapter closes with "The bad news and the good news," one reflection question to carry into prayer, and a brief note from the Reformers. Read it slowly. Read it with your Bible open. Fifty chapters is a journey, not a race — and as you walk it, remember that the people in this book are not marble saints. They are liars and schemers, barren women and forgotten prisoners, men who failed the same way twice. They are people like us. And the God who carried them is carrying you.

---

## How to Use This Commentary

This commentary was built to be used, not just read. Here is how to get the most out of it.

**The order of every session.** Work through each chapter the same way, in this order:

1. **Read the chapter in your own Bible first.** Open your Bible to the chapter of Genesis and read it all the way through before you read a word of commentary. The commentary serves the Scripture; it never replaces it. Genesis was written to be heard, so read it out loud if you can — the genealogies and the dialogues alike. You will catch things with your ears that your eyes slide past.
2. **Then read the commentary section for that chapter.** Keep your Bible open beside it and look up the verses as they come. Start with "Jesus in this chapter" — before anything else, fix your eyes on how Christ is revealed there. The promise of Genesis 3:15 runs under every chapter like a river under a road; learn to listen for it.
3. **Then sit with the reflection question.** Each chapter ends with one reflection question and a "bad news and good news" paragraph. Do not rush past them. Read the bad news honestly — let the law do its searching work — and then read the good news slowly, out loud if you can. Answer the reflection question in prayer, out loud to God, not just in your head.
4. **Write in a notebook.** Keep a simple notebook with this study. Write down your answer to the reflection question, your answer to the study guide question for that chapter (see the Genesis Study Guide below), and the memory verse for the chapter. You might also keep a running page for the promises: every time God repeats His promise to Abraham's family, write it down with its reference. By chapter 50 you will have a list that preaches. You do not need anything fancy — a dollar notebook and a pen will do the whole book.

**The rhythm: three sessions a week.** This fits the study rhythm used across The Hope Connection: three sessions a week, one book at a time, until the book is done. At one chapter per session, three sessions a week, you will walk through all fifty chapters of Genesis in about seventeen weeks. That is a season of your life spent in the book of beginnings — time well spent. If you want to go slower, spread one chapter across the whole week: session one, read the chapter in your Bible and the "Jesus in this chapter" opening; session two, read the commentary; session three, return to the chapter, work through the questions, and pray it back to God. Either pace is fine. What matters is the steadiness, not the speed.

**No catch-up piles.** This rule is not negotiable, because it protects the whole habit: if you miss a day, you do not owe anybody two chapters. You simply pick up at the next chapter at the next session. Life happens — sickness, family, work, hard weeks. A study rhythm that piles up guilt every time life interrupts will not survive a month. This one bends and keeps going. Missed a week? Start again at the next chapter. The Lord is not keeping a tally, and neither are we.

**Using it alone.** Everything above is written for one reader, one Bible, one notebook, and the Lord. Pray before you open the book — a short, real prayer: "Lord, open my eyes, and let this Word search me." Pray again when you finish, turning the chapter's good news into thanksgiving and its bad news into confession.

**Using it with a spouse or a friend.** Read the chapter out loud together at the start of the session — take turns reading. Then read the commentary, or take turns reading it aloud too. Talk through the reflection question and the study guide question together, honestly. Pray for each other out of what the chapter showed you. Genesis is a book about households — God saving families, not just individuals — and two people studying it together will see things one reader alone will miss.

**Using it in a small group.** One chapter per meeting. Have someone read the chapter aloud from the Bible to open. Walk through the commentary section together, stopping wherever the text raises a question. Save real time at the end for the reflection question and the study guide question — that is where study becomes life. Close in prayer, and let the chapter's memory verse be the group's verse for the week: read it together at the start of the next meeting and see who can say it from memory. Keep the group small enough that everybody talks, and keep the door open — Genesis is the Bible's own front door, and every chapter here is written plain enough for a brand-new believer to follow.

**The memory verses.** The study guide below gives one memory verse for each chapter — fifty verses, the spine of Genesis. If you take one verse a week alongside the chapter rhythm, by the time you finish the book you will carry its whole story in your heart: the image of God, the promise to the woman's Seed, the God who sees, the righteousness counted to faith, the LORD who provides, the God who is with you wherever you go, and the God who means evil for good. That is treasure no one can take from you.

---

## Genesis Study Guide

Fifty chapters, fifty sessions (or fifty weeks at the slower pace). For each chapter: the theme, the key verse to watch for, one memory verse to hide in your heart, and one study question to work through in your notebook after the commentary's reflection question.

| Chapter | Theme | Key Verse | Memory Verse (ESV) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Genesis 1 | In the Beginning, God | Genesis 1:1 — God creates all things by His word | "So God created man in his own image, in the image of God he created him; male and female he created them." — Genesis 1:27 |
| Genesis 2 | The Garden and the Marriage | Genesis 2:7 — the LORD forms man and breathes life | "Therefore a man shall leave his father and his mother and hold fast to his wife, and they shall become one flesh." — Genesis 2:24 |
| Genesis 3 | The Fall and the First Promise | Genesis 3:15 — the Seed of the woman | "I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and her offspring; he shall bruise your head, and you shall bruise his heel." — Genesis 3:15 |
| Genesis 4 | Cain and Abel | Genesis 4:10 — the blood of Abel cries out | "If you do well, will you not be accepted? And if you do not do well, sin is crouching at the door. Its desire is contrary to you, but you must rule over it." — Genesis 4:7 |
| Genesis 5 | The Generations of Adam | Genesis 5:24 — Enoch walked with God | "Enoch walked with God, and he was not, for God took him." — Genesis 5:24 |
| Genesis 6 | Noah and the Ark | Genesis 6:8 — Noah found favor | "But Noah found favor in the eyes of the LORD." — Genesis 6:8 |
| Genesis 7 | The Flood | Genesis 7:16 — the LORD shut him in | "And Noah did all that the LORD had commanded him." — Genesis 7:5 |
| Genesis 8 | The Waters Recede | Genesis 8:1 — God remembered Noah | "While the earth remains, seedtime and harvest, cold and heat, summer and winter, day and night, shall not cease." — Genesis 8:22 |
| Genesis 9 | The Covenant with Noah | Genesis 9:13 — the bow in the cloud | "I have set my bow in the cloud, and it shall be a sign of the covenant between me and the earth." — Genesis 9:13 |
| Genesis 10 | The Table of Nations | Genesis 10:32 — the nations spread abroad | "These are the clans of the sons of Noah, according to their genealogies, in their nations, and from these the nations spread abroad on the earth after the flood." — Genesis 10:32 |
| Genesis 11 | Babel and the Line of Shem | Genesis 11:9 — the LORD scattered them | "Therefore its name was called Babel, because there the LORD confused the language of all the earth. And from there the LORD dispersed them over the face of all the earth." — Genesis 11:9 |
| Genesis 12 | The Call of Abram | Genesis 12:3 — all families blessed in Abram | "And I will make of you a great nation, and I will bless you and make your name great, so that you will be a blessing." — Genesis 12:2 |
| Genesis 13 | Abram and Lot Separate | Genesis 13:15 — all the land I give to you | "For all the land that you see I will give to you and to your offspring forever." — Genesis 13:15 |
| Genesis 14 | Melchizedek, King of Salem | Genesis 14:18 — the priest of God Most High | "And blessed be God Most High, who has delivered your enemies into your hand!" — Genesis 14:20 |
| Genesis 15 | Counted as Righteousness | Genesis 15:6 — he believed the LORD | "And he believed the LORD, and he counted it to him as righteousness." — Genesis 15:6 |
| Genesis 16 | Hagar and the God Who Sees | Genesis 16:13 — You are a God of seeing | "So she called the name of the LORD who spoke to her, 'You are a God of seeing,' for she said, 'Truly here I have seen him who looks after me.'" — Genesis 16:13 |
| Genesis 17 | The Covenant of Circumcision | Genesis 17:7 — an everlasting covenant | "And I will establish my covenant between me and you and your offspring after you throughout their generations for an everlasting covenant, to be God to you and to your offspring after you." — Genesis 17:7 |
| Genesis 18 | The Three Visitors and the Intercession | Genesis 18:25 — the Judge of all the earth | "Far be it from you to do such a thing, to put the righteous to death with the wicked, so that the righteous fare as the wicked! Far be that from you! Shall not the Judge of all the earth do what is just?" — Genesis 18:25 |
| Genesis 19 | Sodom Destroyed, Lot Rescued | Genesis 19:29 — God remembered Abraham | "So it was that, when God destroyed the cities of the valley, God remembered Abraham and sent Lot out of the midst of the overthrow when he overthrew the cities in which Lot had lived." — Genesis 19:29 |
| Genesis 20 | Abraham and Abimelech | Genesis 20:17 — Abraham prayed, and God healed | "Then Abraham prayed to God, and God healed Abimelech, and also healed his wife and female slaves so that they bore children." — Genesis 20:17 |
| Genesis 21 | Isaac Born, Ishmael Sent Out | Genesis 21:1 — the LORD visited Sarah | "The LORD visited Sarah as he had said, and the LORD did to Sarah as he had promised." — Genesis 21:1 |
| Genesis 22 | The Binding of Isaac | Genesis 22:14 — the LORD will provide | "So Abraham called the name of that place, 'The LORD will provide'; as it is said to this day, 'On the mount of the LORD it shall be provided.'" — Genesis 22:14 |
| Genesis 23 | The Death of Sarah | Genesis 23:20 — the field made sure to Abraham | "The field and the cave that is in it were made over to Abraham as property for a burying place by the Hittites." — Genesis 23:20 |
| Genesis 24 | A Bride for Isaac | Genesis 24:7 — He will send His angel before you | "The LORD, the God of heaven, who took me from my father's house and from the land of my kindred, and who spoke to me and swore to me, 'To your offspring I will give this land,' he will send his angel before you, and you shall take a wife for my son from there." — Genesis 24:7 |
| Genesis 25 | Abraham's Death and the Twins | Genesis 25:23 — two nations in your womb | "And the LORD said to her, 'Two nations are in your womb, and two peoples from within you shall be divided; the one shall be stronger than the other, the older shall serve the younger.'" — Genesis 25:23 |
| Genesis 26 | Isaac Among the Philistines | Genesis 26:24 — Fear not, I am with you | "And the LORD appeared to him the same night and said, 'I am the God of Abraham your father. Fear not, for I am with you and will bless you and multiply your offspring for my servant Abraham's sake.'" — Genesis 26:24 |
| Genesis 27 | The Blessing Stolen | Genesis 27:29 — peoples serve you, nations bow down | "Let peoples serve you, and nations bow down to you. Be lord over your brothers, and may your mother's sons bow down to you. Cursed be everyone who curses you, and blessed be everyone who blesses you!" — Genesis 27:29 |
| Genesis 28 | The Ladder at Bethel | Genesis 28:15 — I am with you wherever you go | "Behold, I am with you and will keep you wherever you go, and will bring you back to this land. For I will not leave you until I have done what I have promised you." — Genesis 28:15 |
| Genesis 29 | Jacob, Leah, and Rachel | Genesis 29:35 — This time I will praise the LORD | "And she conceived again and bore a son, and said, 'This time I will praise the LORD.' Therefore she called his name Judah. Then she ceased bearing." — Genesis 29:35 |
| Genesis 30 | Children and Wages | Genesis 30:22 — God remembered Rachel | "Then God remembered Rachel, and God listened to her and opened her womb." — Genesis 30:22 |
| Genesis 31 | The Flight from Laban | Genesis 31:3 — Return to the land of your fathers | "Then the LORD said to Jacob, 'Return to the land of your fathers and to your kindred, and I will be with you.'" — Genesis 31:3 |
| Genesis 32 | Wrestling at Peniel | Genesis 32:28 — Your name shall be Israel | "Then he said, 'Your name shall no longer be called Jacob, but Israel, for you have striven with God and with men, and have prevailed.'" — Genesis 32:28 |
| Genesis 33 | Jacob Meets Esau | Genesis 33:20 — an altar, El-Elohe-Israel | "There he erected an altar and called it El-Elohe-Israel." — Genesis 33:20 |
| Genesis 34 | Dinah and the Wrath of Her Brothers | Genesis 34:7 — an outrageous thing in Israel | "But the men were indignant and very angry, because he had done an outrageous thing in Israel by lying with Jacob's daughter, for such a thing must not be done." — Genesis 34:7 |
| Genesis 35 | Back to Bethel | Genesis 35:11 — I am God Almighty | "And God said to him, 'I am God Almighty: be fruitful and multiply. A nation and a company of nations shall come from you, and kings shall come from your own body.'" — Genesis 35:11 |
| Genesis 36 | The Generations of Esau | Genesis 36:8 — Esau settled in Seir | "So Esau settled in the hill country of Seir. (Esau is Edom.)" — Genesis 36:8 |
| Genesis 37 | Joseph's Dreams and the Pit | Genesis 37:28 — sold for twenty shekels | "Then Midianite traders passed by. And they drew Joseph up and lifted him out of the pit, and sold him to the Ishmaelites for twenty shekels of silver. They took Joseph to Egypt." — Genesis 37:28 |
| Genesis 38 | Judah and Tamar | Genesis 38:26 — She is more righteous than I | "Then Judah identified them and said, 'She is more righteous than I, since I did not give her to my son Shelah.' And he did not know her again." — Genesis 38:26 |
| Genesis 39 | Joseph in Potiphar's House | Genesis 39:21 — the LORD was with Joseph | "But the LORD was with Joseph and showed him steadfast love and gave him favor in the sight of the keeper of the prison." — Genesis 39:21 |
| Genesis 40 | The Cupbearer and the Baker | Genesis 40:8 — interpretations belong to God | "They said to him, 'We have had dreams, and there is no one to interpret them.' And Joseph said to them, 'Do not interpretations belong to God? Please tell them to me.'" — Genesis 40:8 |
| Genesis 41 | Pharaoh's Dreams and Joseph Exalted | Genesis 41:16 — It is not in me; God will answer | "Joseph answered Pharaoh, 'It is not in me; God will give Pharaoh a favorable answer.'" — Genesis 41:16 |
| Genesis 42 | The Brothers Come to Egypt | Genesis 42:22 — his blood is now required | "And Reuben answered them, 'Did I not tell you not to sin against the boy? But you did not listen. So now there comes a reckoning for his blood.'" — Genesis 42:22 |
| Genesis 43 | The Second Journey | Genesis 43:23 — Your God has given you treasure | "He replied, 'Peace to you, do not be afraid. Your God and the God of your father has put treasure in your sacks for you. I received your money.' Then he brought Simeon out to them." — Genesis 43:23 |
| Genesis 44 | The Silver Cup and Judah's Plea | Genesis 44:33 — let your servant remain | "Now therefore, please let your servant remain instead of the boy as a servant to my lord, and let the boy go back with his brothers." — Genesis 44:33 |
| Genesis 45 | "I Am Joseph" | Genesis 45:5 — God sent me to preserve life | "And now do not be distressed or angry with yourselves because you sold me here, for God sent me before you to preserve life." — Genesis 45:5 |
| Genesis 46 | Down to Egypt | Genesis 46:3 — Do not be afraid to go down | "Then he said, 'I am God, the God of your father. Do not be afraid to go down to Egypt, for there I will make you into a great nation.'" — Genesis 46:3 |
| Genesis 47 | Jacob Before Pharaoh | Genesis 47:9 — the days of my sojourning | "And Jacob said to Pharaoh, 'The days of the years of my sojourning are 130 years. Few and evil have been the days of the years of my life, and they have not attained to the days of the years of the life of my fathers in the days of their sojourning.'" — Genesis 47:9 |
| Genesis 48 | The Blessing of Ephraim and Manasseh | Genesis 48:15 — the God who has been my shepherd | "And he blessed Joseph and said, 'The God before whom my fathers Abraham and Isaac walked, the God who has been my shepherd all my life long to this day.'" — Genesis 48:15 |
| Genesis 49 | Jacob Blesses His Sons | Genesis 49:10 — the scepter shall not depart from Judah | "The scepter shall not depart from Judah, nor the ruler's staff from between his feet, until tribute comes to him; and to him shall be the obedience of the peoples." — Genesis 49:10 |
| Genesis 50 | "God Meant It for Good" | Genesis 50:20 — you meant evil, God meant it for good | "As for you, you meant evil against me, but God meant it for good, to bring it about that many people should be kept alive, as they are today." — Genesis 50:20 |

### Study Questions — One per Chapter

Work each question in your notebook after the chapter's reflection question. Many of them follow the Bible's own pattern of putting off the old and putting on the new (Ephesians 4:22–24; Colossians 3:8–10).

1. **Genesis 1 — Made in His image.** Before anything was wrong, everything was good, and you were made in God's image. Put off one way you have believed a lie about your worth; put on this practice: thank God out loud today for one specific way He made you on purpose.
2. **Genesis 2 — Not good to be alone.** God built marriage and rest into creation before sin entered. Put off one habit of isolating yourself; put on one step toward honest fellowship — a meal, a call, a confession — this week. Name it.
3. **Genesis 3 — The Seed is promised.** Adam and Eve hid; God came seeking. Put off one place where you are hiding from God; put on coming into the open in prayer today, telling Him exactly where you are, as He asked Adam: "Where are you?"
4. **Genesis 4 — Rule over it.** Sin crouched at Cain's door, and Cain opened it. What sin is crouching at your door right now? Put off feeding it; put on one concrete guard — a door closed, a number blocked, a brother told — this week.
5. **Genesis 5 — Walk with God.** In a chapter of "and he died," Enoch walked with God. Put off one time-wasting pattern; put on a daily walk with God — a set time, a set place — and write down when it will happen.
6. **Genesis 6 — Find favor, obey fully.** Noah found grace and then obeyed in detail for years before rain fell. Put off partial obedience; put on finishing one command of God you have left half-done. Which one?
7. **Genesis 7 — Shut in by God.** Noah obeyed; God shut the door. Put off anxiety over the doors in your life; put on trust: name one situation you will leave in God's hands this week instead of forcing.
8. **Genesis 8 — God remembers.** "God remembered Noah" did not mean He had forgotten. Put off the belief that your waiting means God's absence; put on building an altar — one deliberate act of thanksgiving for one mercy in the waiting.
9. **Genesis 9 — Look at the bow.** God hung His war bow in the clouds, pointing away from the earth. Put off fear that God's anger is aimed at you in Christ; put on looking up: the next time judgment anxiety rises, say Genesis 9:13 out loud.
10. **Genesis 10 — Every nation matters.** God kept the names of seventy nations, not just one family. Put off a narrow heart; put on praying this week for one nation or people group by name — and for one neighbor not like you.
11. **Genesis 11 — Babel or blessing.** Men built to make a name for themselves; God gave Abram a name by grace. Put off one project of self-naming; put on receiving: write down one gift from God you did nothing to earn, and thank Him for it.
12. **Genesis 12 — Go, and be a blessing.** Abram left everything on a promise. Put off one security you are clutching; put on one act of blessing toward "all families" — someone outside your usual circle — this week.
13. **Genesis 13 — Choose like Abram.** Lot chose by sight and drifted toward Sodom; Abram chose peace and trusted the promise. Put off choosing by appetite; put on choosing by promise: name one decision before you now and how the promise, not the view, will decide it.
14. **Genesis 14 — Refuse the spoil.** Abram took bread and wine from Melchizedek and refused riches from Sodom's king. Put off one gain that would own you; put on tithe-hearted generosity: give first to God this week before spending on yourself.
15. **Genesis 15 — Believe the LORD.** Righteousness was counted to Abram because he believed, not because he performed. Put off one place where you are trying to earn God's acceptance; put on resting: say Genesis 15:6 over yourself in prayer today.
16. **Genesis 16 — The God who sees.** Hagar, a runaway slave, named God "You are a God of seeing." Put off believing you are invisible in your trouble; put on calling on El Roi by name in prayer — and looking for one overlooked person to see this week.
17. **Genesis 17 — Walk before Me.** God renamed Abram at ninety-nine, when everything looked impossible. Put off the deadline you have set on God's promises; put on walking before Him today in one ordinary duty done as unto Him.
18. **Genesis 18 — Plead for the city.** Abraham stood before the LORD and pleaded for Sodom. Put off writing off a person or place as too far gone; put on intercession: pray by name this week for someone others have given up on.
19. **Genesis 19 — Don't look back.** Lot's wife looked back and froze. Put off one longing glance at the life God is bringing you out of; put on running: name the mercy carrying you (Genesis 19:29 says God remembered) and thank Him for it.
20. **Genesis 20 — Grace after failure.** Abraham repeated an old sin, and God still guarded the promise. Put off despair over a repeated failure; put on fresh repentance — and one safeguard so the same fear does not drive the same sin again.
21. **Genesis 21 — Laughter arrives.** Sarah laughed in unbelief in chapter 18 and in joy in chapter 21. Put off cynicism about God's timing; put on keeping a written list of prayers you are still waiting on — and one line of thanks for the ones already answered.
22. **Genesis 22 — The LORD will provide.** Abraham surrendered the dearest thing and found a substitute provided. Put off withholding your "Isaac" — the person, plan, or possession you trust more than God; put on open hands in prayer today, naming it and releasing it to Him.
23. **Genesis 23 — Faith buys a grave.** Abraham's first owned land in Canaan was a tomb: he believed God while burying his wife. Put off demanding to see the whole promise now; put on one act of faith in a place that looks like loss — a prayer, a gift, a word of hope spoken over it.
24. **Genesis 24 — Guided step by step.** The servant prayed a specific prayer and watched God lead. Put off anxious control of a decision; put on the servant's way: pray specifically, then take the next open step and note how God answers.
25. **Genesis 25 — Don't despise the birthright.** Esau sold forever for a stew. Put off one appetite that trades the lasting for the immediate; put on remembering before you are hungry — decide now, in writing, what you will do when that appetite speaks.
26. **Genesis 26 — Dig again.** Isaac reopened his father's wells instead of fighting over them. Put off contention over a stopped-up well in your life; put on digging again: return to one neglected practice of grace — prayer, the Word, fellowship — this week.
27. **Genesis 27 — The blessing and the deceit.** God carried His purpose through a deceitful scheme without excusing the deceit. Put off scheming to get God's gifts your way; put on honesty: confess one manipulation, large or small, to God — and to the person if needed.
28. **Genesis 28 — God in this place.** Jacob woke in an ordinary field and found it the house of God. Put off the belief that God is only in special places; put on awareness: tonight, name the ordinary place where God met you today.
29. **Genesis 29 — Judah from Leah.** The royal line came through the unloved wife who learned to praise. Put off measuring your worth by being chosen or noticed; put on Leah's word — "This time I will praise the LORD" — and praise Him for one unnoticed mercy.
30. **Genesis 30 — God remembers.** Rachel's years of waiting ended with "God remembered Rachel." Put off the scorekeeping of a long wait; put on asking God plainly for the desire of your heart, and leaving the timing written in His hands, not yours.
31. **Genesis 31 — God has seen my affliction.** Jacob testified that God saw and rebuked on his behalf. Put off rehearsing a wrong done to you in order to repay it; put on committing your case to God in prayer — and leaving Laban's territory, in heart if not in geography.
32. **Genesis 32 — Wrestle and limp.** Jacob came away blessed and limping. Put off demanding that God bless you without changing you; put on clinging: in prayer this week, refuse to let go of one promise of God until you have His blessing on it.
33. **Genesis 33 — The face of God in a brother.** Jacob saw God's face in Esau's embrace. Put off delaying a reconciliation; put on going first: name one person, and take the first step toward peace this week, however small.
34. **Genesis 34 — Grief without revenge.** Dinah's brothers answered evil with worse evil. Put off answering wrong with wrong; put on grieving honestly before God — bring one injustice to Him in prayer and leave the vengeance in His hands (Deuteronomy 32:35).
35. **Genesis 35 — Put away the foreign gods.** Jacob's household buried its idols under an oak before going up to Bethel. Put off keeping one household idol — object, habit, or subscription; put on burying it: literally remove it this week, and go up to worship.
36. **Genesis 36 — God's kindness to Esau's line.** Edom had kings while Israel was still a family. Put off envy when others advance faster; put on thanking God for one blessing in the life of someone whose path is not yours.
37. **Genesis 37 — The beloved son betrayed.** Joseph's brothers hated the favored son. Put off envy of a favored brother or sister; put on this: if you are the one in the pit, write down Joseph's story in one sentence — betrayed, not abandoned — and pray it back to God.
38. **Genesis 38 — Judah confesses.** Judah's turning began with three honest words' worth of confession: "She is more righteous than I." Put off covering a shameful chapter; put on Judah's confession — admit your part plainly to God, and where needed, to the person wronged.
39. **Genesis 39 — The LORD was with Joseph.** In a prison Joseph kept his integrity and his usefulness. Put off the belief that faithfulness only counts in good circumstances; put on doing excellent, honest work in the place you are — as unto the LORD who is with you.
40. **Genesis 40 — Forgotten, not forsaken.** The cupbearer forgot Joseph for two full years. Put off bitterness at being forgotten by people; put on remembering that God's memory is not man's — write down one way God has remembered you while men forgot.
41. **Genesis 41 — It is not in me.** Joseph refused the credit and gave God the answer. Put off claiming glory for God's gifts in you; put on Joseph's sentence — "It is not in me" — and use it, in some form, the next time you are praised.
42. **Genesis 42 — Guilt remembered.** Twenty years on, the brothers' guilt woke up. Put off burying an old sin deeper; put on bringing it up — confess one long-ago wrong to God today, and if the person still lives and it is safe, begin to make it right.
43. **Genesis 43 — Treasure in the sacks.** The brothers feared a kindness they did not understand. Put off suspecting every mercy; put on receiving grace as grace: name one unearned kindness in your life and thank the Giver without trying to repay it.
44. **Genesis 44 — Judah stands in the gap.** The man who sold Joseph now offers himself as a slave for Benjamin. Put off protecting yourself at a brother's expense; put on standing in the gap: name one person you can shield, vouch for, or carry this week — and do it.
45. **Genesis 45 — God sent me.** Joseph wept, forgave, and fed the brothers who sold him. Put off rehearsing the betrayal; put on Joseph's theology: write down the worst thing done to you, and beside it, one way God has used it to preserve life.
46. **Genesis 46 — Do not be afraid to go down.** God met Jacob at Beersheba before the descent into Egypt. Put off fear of the next season; put on worship before you move: stop at your own Beersheba — pray and offer thanks before the change, not only after it.
47. **Genesis 47 — A sojourner's honesty.** Jacob called his years "few and evil" and still blessed Pharaoh. Put off both complaining and pretending; put on Jacob's honesty: tell God the truth about your years today, and then bless someone from inside the hardship.
48. **Genesis 48 — The Shepherd all my life long.** Dying Jacob's summary of God was one word: Shepherd. Put off summarizing your life by its worst chapters; put on Jacob's summary: write your own one-sentence testimony of God's shepherding, and keep it in your Bible.
49. **Genesis 49 — The scepter and the blessing.** Jacob's blessings were prophecy: Judah's line would carry the King. Put off a small view of your words to the next generation; put on blessing: speak one deliberate, Scripture-shaped blessing over a child, grandchild, or young believer this week.
50. **Genesis 50 — God meant it for good.** Joseph died trusting a promise he would not live to see kept. Put off demanding the final chapter now; put on Joseph's faith: name one unfinished promise you will entrust to God beyond your own lifetime — and rest.

---

## Genesis 1 — In the Beginning, God

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is not an afterthought added to the story later. He is there at the start. John opens his Gospel by reaching back to this chapter: "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God... All things were made through him" (John 1:1, 3). The Father creates, the Spirit hovers over the waters, and the Son is the Word through whom everything is made. When God says, "Let there be light," we are hearing the voice that would one day stand in Galilee and say, "I am the light of the world" (John 8:12).

That means creation itself is personal. The world was not made by a distant force and left to run on its own. It was made by the triune God who would later enter His own creation to rescue it. Colossians says of Christ, "by him all things were created... and in him all things hold together" (Colossians 1:16–17). The hands that formed the stars are the same hands that would be stretched out on a cross. If you feel like your life is formless and empty, like the earth in verse 2, take heart: the God who spoke order out of chaos in Genesis 1 is the same Lord who makes all things new in Christ.

### Commentary

**Before anything was made (1:1).** The Bible does not begin with an argument for God's existence. It begins with God. "In the beginning, God created the heavens and the earth." Before there was time, matter, or a single human life, God was. That one sentence rules out every rival story. The universe is not eternal, it is not an accident, and it is not God. It was made, on purpose, by a God who was already there. For men and women whose past feels like it defines them, this is good ground to stand on: your beginning is not the beginning. God was before your first failure, and He will be after your last one.

The Hebrew word for the opening phrase is *bereshit*, "in the beginning," and the word for God's creating here is *bara* — a word used in Scripture only with God as its subject. Men make things out of what exists. God creates out of nothing, by the power of His word alone. He does not struggle, strain, or run out of strength. He speaks, and it is so.

**Formless, empty, and dark (1:2).** The earth starts "without form and void," covered in darkness, with the Spirit of God "hovering over the face of the waters." This is not evil; it is unfinished. God is present before anything looks ordered. Many people first meet God exactly there — in a life that feels empty, dark, and shapeless. The Spirit hovers before the light ever breaks. God does not wait for us to tidy up before He draws near. He is near at the very start of the work.

**The creation of light (1:3–5).** God's first spoken work is light. "And God said, 'Let there be light,' and there was light." He separates light from darkness and calls the light Day and the darkness Night. Notice: God names things. Naming is authority. The darkness is real, but it does not get the final word, and it does not get to name itself. God sets its boundary. Paul later uses this very moment to describe conversion: "God, who said, 'Let light shine out of darkness,' has shone in our hearts" (2 Corinthians 4:6). The first day teaches us how every new life begins — God speaks light into darkness that could never produce light on its own.

**Sky, waters, and dry land (1:6–10).** On the second day God separates the waters and makes the expanse, the sky. On the third day the waters are gathered so dry land appears, and God calls the land Earth and the gathered waters Seas. He is making a place fit for life — ordered, bounded, and safe. The sea in Scripture often stands for chaos and threat, but here the sea only goes where God tells it to go. That matters for anxious hearts: the chaos in your life is not in charge. God sets limits even to the flood.

**Earth bringing forth life (1:11–13).** The land produces vegetation, plants yielding seed, and fruit trees, each "according to its kind." God builds fruitfulness and continuity into creation. Seeds carry tomorrow inside them. Recovery, growth, and sanctification work much the same way — rarely all at once, usually seed by seed, season by season, according to God's kind and steady design. He is not in a hurry, and He is never late.

**Lights in the heavens (1:14–19).** On the fourth day God sets the sun, moon, and stars as signs for seasons, days, and years, to give light on the earth and to rule the day and the night. In the ancient world, nations worshiped the sun and moon as gods. Genesis quietly demotes them: they are lamps God hung, not lords anyone should bow to. They serve. They mark time for farmers, sailors, and families. Your calendar — your days and years — belongs to God, not to the stars, not to luck, and not to your past.

**Living creatures in water and sky (1:20–23).** On the fifth day the waters swarm with living creatures and birds fly above the earth. Here God gives His first blessing: "Be fruitful and multiply." Blessing is not something man invented or earned. It flows from God's own mouth toward creatures He delights in. Life is a gift before it is ever a task.

**Animals, and man in God's image (1:24–28).** The sixth day begins with livestock, creeping things, and beasts of the earth, each according to its kind. Then comes the crown of the chapter. God says, "Let us make man in our image, after our likeness." The plural has long been read by the church as a hint of the Trinity — Father, Son, and Holy Spirit in counsel — and at the very least it shows this act is deliberate and weighty. Mankind, male and female, is made in God's image. That image is not earned by success, lost by addiction, or erased by a prison record. It is given in creation. Dominion follows: man is to rule the earth as God's steward, not as a tyrant — caring for what God made the way God Himself cares for it. And God blesses them and commands fruitfulness. Your worth was settled here, before you ever did anything right or wrong.

**Very good (1:29–31).** God gives every green plant for food and looks over everything He has made: "and behold, it was very good." Not barely acceptable. Not a rough draft. Very good. The chapter ends with evening and morning, the sixth day. If shame has taught you to call yourself worthless, Genesis 1 disagrees with shame. The God who made you called His work very good, and He has not changed His mind about the value of the people He made in His image.

### Key verses

> "In the beginning, God created the heavens and the earth." — Genesis 1:1 (ESV)

> "And God said, 'Let there be light,' and there was light." — Genesis 1:3 (ESV)

> "Then God said, 'Let us make man in our image, after our likeness. And let them have dominion over the fish of the sea and over the birds of the heavens and over the livestock and over all the earth and over every creeping thing that creeps on the earth.'" — Genesis 1:26 (ESV)

> "So God created man in his own image, in the image of God he created him; male and female he created them." — Genesis 1:27 (ESV)

> "And God saw everything that he had made, and behold, it was very good. And there was evening and there was morning, the sixth day." — Genesis 1:31 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news in this chapter is quiet but real: we did not make ourselves, we do not belong to ourselves, and a God this great has a rightful claim on every life He made — which means none of us gets to be our own god, write our own rules, or pretend He is not there. Left to ourselves, we live as if the first four words of the Bible were not true. The good news is that this Creator is not cold or far off: the God who spoke light into darkness sent His Son, the true Light, into our darkness, and in Christ He is still speaking — creating new hearts, making new people, and promising a day when He will make all things new again. The hands that made you are the hands that remake you.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther, in his Lectures on Genesis, lingered over verse 1 with real joy, teaching that Moses begins by giving us a God who creates out of nothing by His word alone — and that this same powerful Word is the one that comforts troubled consciences, because the God who needed no material to make the world needs nothing in us to begin His work of grace. Calvin likewise stressed that creation is a theatre of God's glory, meant to leave men without excuse and to lead them to know and trust their Maker.

**Reflection question:** Where does your life feel formless, empty, or dark right now — and what would it mean to trust that the Spirit is already hovering there, and that God can speak light into it?

## Genesis 2 — The Garden and the Marriage

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 2 gives us two pictures that find their full meaning in Christ. The first is rest. God finishes His work and rests on the seventh day, and Jesus later stands in front of weary people and says, "Come to me, all who labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest" (Matthew 11:28). The Sabbath of Genesis 2 is a shadow; Jesus is the substance. True rest is not just a day off. It is a Person, and it is found when our striving is finished in His finished work.

The second picture is marriage. Adam's cry when he sees Eve — "This at last is bone of my bones and flesh of my flesh" — and the joining of husband and wife as "one flesh" point beyond Eden. Paul quotes Genesis 2:24 in Ephesians 5 and says plainly, "This mystery is profound, and I am saying that it refers to Christ and the church" (Ephesians 5:32). Marriage was always meant to be a living picture of Christ's covenant love for His bride. And notice how Eve is formed: from Adam's opened side while he sleeps. The church has long seen a gentle foreshadowing there — the bride of the second Adam born, as it were, from His pierced side on the cross.

### Commentary

**God rests and blesses the seventh day (2:1–3).** The heavens and the earth are finished, and God rests — not because He is tired, but because the work is complete and good. He blesses the seventh day and makes it holy, setting it apart. Rest, then, is built into creation itself. It is not a reward for people who have earned it or a luxury for people who can afford it. Before sin, before striving, before any of our drivenness, God wove rest into the rhythm of the world. A man who cannot stop working is not being more spiritual than God. He is forgetting he is a creature.

**The man formed and placed (2:4–7).** The focus narrows from the whole cosmos to one man and one garden. "The LORD God formed the man of dust from the ground and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life." We are dust and breath at the same time — lowly in our origin, dignified by God's own breath. That combination keeps us humble and hopeful at once. You are not an animal and you are not an angel. You are dust that God Himself breathed into, made to live before His face.

**The garden of Eden (2:8–14).** God plants a garden in Eden, in the east, and puts the man there. It is a place of beauty and provision: every tree pleasant to the sight and good for food, a river watering the garden and dividing into four rivers that reach far into the world. God's generosity is on full display. Eden is not bare survival. It is abundance, order, and delight. The four rivers remind us that God's blessing in Eden was never meant to stay fenced in — it flows outward to the nations, the way grace still does.

**The tree of life and the tree of knowledge (2:9, 16–17).** In the middle of the garden stand two trees. The tree of life speaks of life with God, freely offered. The tree of the knowledge of good and evil carries the one command and the one warning: "You may surely eat of every tree of the garden, but of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat, for in the day that you eat of it you shall surely die." Notice the proportion: a whole garden of yes, and a single no. God is not stingy. The command is not a trap; it is the shape of trust. Adam is free, and his freedom is proved and exercised by glad obedience to one clear word from a good God.

**Work before the fall (2:15).** "The LORD God took the man and put him in the garden of Eden to work it and keep it." Work is not part of the curse. It comes before sin ever enters. Adam is a gardener and a guardian — working and keeping, the same pair of words later used for the priests who served in God's tabernacle. Eden is, in a sense, God's first sanctuary, and Adam its first priest. Your daily work, done before God, has dignity. Sweeping a floor, turning a wrench, sitting at a desk — offered to the Lord, it is holy ground.

**It is not good that man should be alone (2:18–20).** For the first time in the creation story, God says something is "not good" — and it is man's aloneness. God brings the animals to Adam, and Adam names them, showing real authority and wisdom, but among them "there was not found a helper fit for him." The lesson lands softly: human companionship is a need God Himself named. We were not built to heal, grow, or follow Christ alone. Isolation is not strength. God said so first.

**The woman built, the marriage given (2:21–25).** God causes a deep sleep to fall on Adam, takes one of his ribs, and builds the woman — close to his heart, from his side: not from his head to rule over him, nor from his foot to be trampled by him, but from his side, to be beside him. Adam wakes and sings the first poem in the Bible. Then Moses gives the pattern for every marriage after: "Therefore a man shall leave his father and his mother and hold fast to his wife, and they shall become one flesh." Leave, hold fast, become one — a new covenant family, a permanent bond, a total union. The chapter closes with a line full of innocence we have all lost and long for: "And the man and his wife were both naked and were not ashamed." No hiding. No fear. Nothing to cover. That is what sin will steal in the next chapter — and what Christ came to restore.

### Key verses

> "And on the seventh day God finished his work that he had done, and he rested on the seventh day from all his work that he had done." — Genesis 2:2 (ESV)

> "then the LORD God formed the man of dust from the ground and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and the man became a living creature." — Genesis 2:7 (ESV)

> "Then the LORD God said, 'It is not good that the man should be alone; I will make him a helper fit for him.'" — Genesis 2:18 (ESV)

> "Therefore a man shall leave his father and his mother and hold fast to his wife, and they shall become one flesh." — Genesis 2:24 (ESV)

> "And the man and his wife were both naked and were not ashamed." — Genesis 2:25 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that the rest, the openness, and the unashamed closeness of this chapter describe a world we no longer live in: we are restless workers who cannot stop, husbands and wives who hide from each other, and lonely people surrounded by crowds, because sin has broken what God called good. The good news is that Jesus restores both gifts — He gives true Sabbath rest to heavy-laden sinners who come to Him, and He takes the church as His own bride, holding fast to her at the cost of His life, so that in Him we are known fully, covered completely, and never ashamed before God again.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, commenting on this chapter, emphasized that God's resting was set before us as a pattern — that men should cease from their own works and let God work in them, which he saw as the heart of Sabbath rest and a picture of the believer's rest in grace. Luther delighted in the marriage passage, preaching that marriage is God's own good institution from paradise, not a lesser spiritual state, and that the "one flesh" union is His design for mutual help, comfort, and holiness in ordinary daily life.

**Reflection question:** Which gift of this chapter do you most need to receive again from Christ — true rest from striving, or freedom from hiding and shame — and what one step toward it can you take this week?

## Genesis 3 — The Fall and the First Promise

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 3 is the darkest chapter in the Bible so far — and it contains the first promise of Jesus. In the middle of the curse, God speaks to the serpent and declares war: the offspring of the woman will crush the serpent's head, though his own heel will be bruised in the striking. The church has called Genesis 3:15 the *protoevangelium*, the first gospel. It is vague — no name, no date, no town — but it is real: a descendant of Eve is coming who will undo what the serpent did, at cost to himself. Every promise after this one is a narrowing of this one, until the angel tells Mary she will bear a son.

Jesus is also seen in the chapter's mercy. God seeks the hiding couple with a question, "Where are you?" — the same seeking heart Jesus described when He said He came "to seek and to save the lost" (Luke 19:10). And when Adam and Eve stand in fig leaves of their own making, God clothes them Himself with garments of skins — something had to die so they could be covered. It is the first picture of a truth the whole Bible will unfold: sinners cannot cover their own shame, but God provides a covering, through death, at His own cost. In Christ, we are clothed not in skins but in righteousness itself (Galatians 3:27).

### Commentary

**The serpent's question (3:1).** The serpent is "more crafty than any other beast of the field," and he opens not with a command but with a question: "Did God actually say, 'You shall not eat of any tree in the garden'?" He exaggerates God's command to make God look harsh, and he plants doubt about God's word and God's goodness at the same time. That is still the shape of temptation. It rarely begins with open rebellion. It begins with a slanted version of what God said and a quiet suggestion that God is holding out on you.

**The woman's answer and the added line (3:2–3).** Eve answers mostly rightly — they may eat of the trees, only one is forbidden — but she adds a line God did not say: "neither shall you touch it." Small distortions matter. When we soften or sharpen God's word, we lose our footing. Still, the heart of her answer is sound: God has been generous, and the prohibition is narrow. Temptation works best when we forget the generosity and stare only at the one forbidden tree.

**The lie (3:4–5).** "You will not surely die." The serpent flatly contradicts God and then offers the real bait: "You will be like God, knowing good and evil." The promise is autonomy — to decide good and evil for yourself instead of receiving them from God. Every fall since has followed this pattern: disbelieve the warning, doubt the goodness, reach for godhood. The tragedy is that Adam and Eve were already like God, made in His image. They traded what they had for a lie about what they lacked.

**The eating (3:6).** Eve saw the tree was good for food, a delight to the eyes, and desired to make one wise — and she ate, and gave to her husband, who was with her, and he ate. The senses, the appetite, and the ambition are all engaged. Adam's silence is part of his sin: he was given the command directly and the charge to guard the garden, and he stood by. Sin is rarely only private. It is handed on, shared, and multiplied — at kitchen tables, in marriages, in families. That is why the way back must also be personal and real.

**Eyes opened, shame born (3:7–8).** Their eyes are opened — but not to glory. They know they are naked, sew fig leaves together, and hide from God among the trees when they hear Him walking in the garden. The knowledge they grabbed brought shame, not godhood. Fig leaves are man's first religion: self-made covering, itchy and inadequate. And hiding is man's first response to guilt. If you have ever avoided prayer because you felt too dirty to pray, you know this garden scene from the inside.

**God seeks and questions (3:9–13).** God comes asking, "Where are you?" — not because He lacks information, but because He is drawing Adam out of hiding. His questions give Adam room to confess. Instead, the blame begins: the man blames the woman — and God who gave her ("The woman whom you gave to be with me"); the woman blames the serpent. Sin always looks for somewhere else to live. Confession dies where blame thrives. Yet note God's patience: He questions each one. He does not strike them dead on the spot, though He warned of death. Judgment is real, but mercy is already moving.

**The curse on the serpent and the promise (3:14–15).** God curses the serpent to crawl on its belly and eat dust — a picture of humiliation and defeat. Then comes the promise inside the curse: "I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and her offspring; he shall bruise your head, and you shall bruise his heel." Head and heel: one wound is fatal, the other is real but not final. At the cross, Satan bruised Christ's heel in suffering and death — but Christ crushed the serpent's head, disarming the powers and breaking sin's claim (Colossians 2:15). The whole rest of the Bible is the outworking of this one verse.

**The consequences for woman and man (3:16–19).** Pain enters childbearing, conflict enters marriage, and thorns enter work. The ground is cursed because of Adam; his labor will be painful toil until he returns to the dust he was taken from: "for you are dust, and to dust you shall return." These are not petty punishments. They describe life east of Eden honestly — the pain in our bodies, our homes, and our jobs that every reader recognizes. God tells the truth about what sin costs. He does not pretend the fall changed nothing.

**A name of faith, garments of grace (3:20–21).** In the shadow of death, Adam names his wife Eve, "because she was the mother of all living" — an act of faith in God's promise that life, and a Deliverer, will come through her line. Then God clothes them with garments of skins. Their fig leaves would not do. God's covering would. Before He sends them out, He covers them. Grace before exile.

**Sent out, guarded way (3:22–24).** God sends them from the garden "lest he reach out his hand and take also of the tree of life and live forever" in a fallen state — which is itself mercy, not cruelty. To live forever in sin and shame would be hell, not heaven. Cherubim and a flaming sword guard the way to the tree of life. The way is closed — for now. In Revelation, the tree of life stands open again in the city of God, its leaves for the healing of the nations (Revelation 22:2). What was barred in Genesis 3 is reopened through the bruised and risen Seed.

### Key verses

> "But the LORD God called to the man and said to him, 'Where are you?'" — Genesis 3:9 (ESV)

> "I will put enmity between you and the woman, and between your offspring and her offspring; he shall bruise your head, and you shall bruise his heel." — Genesis 3:15 (ESV)

> "And the LORD God made for Adam and for his wife garments of skins and clothed them." — Genesis 3:21 (ESV)

> "Therefore the LORD God sent him out from the garden of Eden to work the ground from which he was taken." — Genesis 3:23 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that sin is not a mistake or a sickness only — it is rebellion against a good God, and it brought exactly what He warned: death, shame, blame, pain in our homes and work, and exile from His presence; every one of us is born east of Eden, hiding in our own fig leaves. The good news is that God came looking for the hiders, promised a Seed of the woman who would crush the serpent at the cost of His own bruising, and covered the guilty with a covering He provided — and all of it came true in Jesus Christ, who was bruised for us at the cross, rose to crush death itself, and now clothes everyone who trusts Him in His own righteousness and brings them back to God.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther treated Genesis 3:15 as a treasure — the first and grandest promise of Christ, preached, as he saw it, right in paradise to people who had just ruined everything, so that Adam and Eve lived the rest of their lives by faith in a Deliverer they had never seen. Calvin emphasized God's seeking question and His clothing of the couple as proof that even in judgment God was acting as a Father, restraining His wrath and giving sinners room to hope in the promised Seed rather than despair.

**Reflection question:** Where are you still hiding from God behind blame or fig leaves — and what would it look like today to answer His "Where are you?" honestly and let Him clothe you?

## Genesis 4 — Cain and Abel

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus stands in this chapter by contrast, in the blood of a righteous man. Abel offered a better sacrifice than Cain (Hebrews 11:4), was murdered for his righteousness, and his blood, Genesis says, cries out from the ground. The writer to the Hebrews sets Abel and Jesus side by side: believers have come "to Jesus, the mediator of a new covenant, and to the sprinkled blood that speaks a better word than the blood of Abel" (Hebrews 12:24). Abel's blood cried out for justice against his murderer. Jesus' blood cries out for pardon for His murderers — "Father, forgive them" — and for everyone who trusts in Him.

Jesus also identified Himself with Abel. He spoke of "the blood of righteous Abel" as the first in a long line of murdered witnesses (Matthew 23:35), and He warned that the spirit of Cain — envy of a brother's acceptance with God — was alive in the religious leaders who would kill Him. Cain is the Bible's first portrait of a man who would rather murder righteousness than repent of sin. Christ is the opposite portrait: the righteous Brother who was murdered, and who used His death not to curse His killers but to save sinners, including killers who come to Him in repentance.

### Commentary

**Two sons, two callings (4:1–2).** Eve bears Cain and says, "I have gotten a man with the help of the LORD" — hope is still alive after the fall; she may even wonder if this son is the promised Seed. Then Abel is born. Cain works the ground; Abel keeps sheep. Both callings are honest. The difference between the brothers is not their work but their worship, and the chapter will show that what happens at the altar reveals what is happening in the heart.

**Two offerings (4:3–5).** In the course of time Cain brings an offering of the fruit of the ground, and Abel brings the firstborn of his flock and their fat portions. "And the LORD had regard for Abel and his offering, but for Cain and his offering he had no regard." Scripture does not leave us guessing about the reason: Hebrews tells us Abel offered his sacrifice "by faith" (Hebrews 11:4), and 1 John tells us Cain's works were evil while his brother's were righteous (1 John 3:12). God is not impressed by religious activity without faith and a heart turned toward Him. Cain gave something. Abel gave the first and the best, in trust. Outwardly both men worshiped. Inwardly, only one came God's way.

**God's warning to an angry man (4:5–7).** Cain is "very angry, and his face fell." What God does next is remarkable: He comes to Cain — before any murder — with a question and a warning. "Why are you angry, and why has your face fallen? If you do well, will you not be accepted? And if you do not do well, sin is crouching at the door. Its desire is contrary to you, but you must rule over it." Sin is pictured as a beast crouching, ready to spring. God warns Cain while there is still time. That is the mercy of God toward a man on the edge: a clear word, an open door back, and an honest warning that the thing he is nursing will master him if he will not master it by God's help. Resentment left alone does not stay small.

**The murder in the field (4:8).** Cain speaks to Abel, and when they are in the field, Cain rises up against his brother and kills him. The first death in human history is not by old age or accident. It is a murder — brother killing brother, in private, after a warning from God Himself. Sin that began with eating forbidden fruit in chapter 3 has grown, in one generation, into bloodshed. That is sin's trajectory whenever it is entertained rather than confessed. It always takes more than it first asked for.

**"Am I my brother's keeper?" (4:9–10).** God asks Cain, "Where is Abel your brother?" — the same seeking style He used with Adam. Cain lies and shrugs: "I do not know; am I my brother's keeper?" The answer to his question, woven through the whole Bible, is yes — we are given to one another, responsible to love and guard our brothers and sisters, not to envy or erase them. Then God declares that Abel's blood cries out from the ground. Murder cannot be buried deep enough to silence it. God hears the blood of every victim. No one is unseen, no wrong unheard, even when no human court ever takes the case.

**Cain's curse and Cain's mark (4:11–16).** Cain is cursed from the ground: the soil he worked will no longer yield to him, and he will be "a fugitive and a wanderer on the earth." Cain cries that his punishment is greater than he can bear — he fears being killed himself. Even here there is a strange mercy: God puts a mark on Cain to protect him from vengeance, and decrees sevenfold vengeance on anyone who kills him. God restrains the cycle of revenge even for a murderer. Cain then goes away "from the presence of the LORD" and settles in the land of Nod — wandering, east of Eden, farther from God. It is one of the saddest sentences in Genesis. A man warned by God, protected by God, who still walks away from God's presence rather than repent.

**Cain's line: skill and violence (4:17–24).** Cain's descendants build cities, raise livestock, make music, and forge tools — real cultural gifts, even in a line under curse. But the line climaxes with Lamech, who takes two wives and sings a boastful song of revenge: "If Cain's revenge is sevenfold, then Lamech's is seventy-sevenfold." Violence has become a point of pride. Civilization advances and the heart does not. Skill without repentance builds bigger weapons. This is where the line of Cain leads: talent, pride, and blood.

**Another son, a new beginning (4:25–26).** The chapter does not end with Lamech. God gives Adam and Eve another son, Seth — "God has appointed for me another offspring instead of Abel, for Cain killed him." Eve's hope is not in Cain's line but in God's appointment. Seth has a son, Enosh, and the chapter closes with a quiet, golden line: "At that time people began to call upon the name of the LORD." Against the backdrop of murder and boasting, a people begins to pray. The line of promise runs not through the strong and violent, but through those who call on the name of the Lord. It still does.

### Key verses

> "If you do well, will you not be accepted? And if you do not do well, sin is crouching at the door. Its desire is contrary to you, but you must rule over it." — Genesis 4:7 (ESV)

> "Then the LORD said to Cain, 'Where is Abel your brother?' He said, 'I do not know; am I my brother's keeper?'" — Genesis 4:9 (ESV)

> "And the LORD said, 'What have you done? The voice of your brother's blood is crying to me from the ground.'" — Genesis 4:10 (ESV)

> "To Seth also a son was born, and he called his name Enosh. At that time people began to call upon the name of the LORD." — Genesis 4:26 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that sin crouches at every door: envy of a brother, anger nursed instead of confessed, and violence that grows from a fallen face to a bloody field in a single generation — and Abel's blood, like all innocent blood, cries out to God for justice, a cry none of us could answer for our own sins. The good news is that there is better blood than Abel's: the blood of Jesus, the righteous Brother who was slain by envious men, speaks not vengeance but pardon, cleansing, and peace with God for all who call upon His name — so that even those with Cain-like pasts can stop wandering, come back into God's presence, and be received.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, on this chapter, pressed the difference between the brothers' offerings into a doctrine of worship: God is not worshiped rightly by outward gifts alone, but first by faith and a pure heart, and he warned that Cain's anger at God's regard for Abel is the perennial temptation of hypocrites, who would rather rage at grace than submit to it. Spurgeon often preached Abel's blood and Christ's blood together — Abel's crying for vengeance, Christ's crying for mercy — and urged sinners that the very blood that condemns their sin in Abel's case pleads for their pardon in Christ's.

**Reflection question:** What anger or envy is crouching at your door right now — and will you bring it to God in confession today, while His warning is still an open door back?

## Genesis 5 — The Generations of Adam

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 5 is a genealogy — a list of names, ages, and deaths — and yet Christ is the reason the list exists. This is the line of Seth, the appointed line from chapter 4, carried name by name toward Noah, and eventually, through the rest of Scripture's genealogies, to Abraham, to David, and to Jesus Himself. Luke's Gospel traces Christ's ancestry back through this very chapter: "the son of Enosh, the son of Seth, the son of Adam, the son of God" (Luke 3:38). Every name here is a link in a chain God refused to let break. The Seed promised to Eve in Genesis 3:15 would come through real people, in real families, across real centuries.

Jesus is also present by contrast in the chapter's one shining exception. Enoch "walked with God, and he was not, for God took him." In a list where every other entry ends "and he died," Enoch's entry ends in life. He is a foretaste — a living hint that death is not the final word for those who walk with God. Jesus makes that hint a promise kept: He rose bodily from the grave, and He says to His people, "Because I live, you also will live" (John 14:19). Enoch shows the direction. Jesus opens the door for the whole family of faith to follow.

### Commentary

**The book of the generations of Adam (5:1–2).** The chapter opens by looking backward: "This is the book of the generations of Adam. When God created man, he made him in the likeness of God. Male and female he created them, and he blessed them and named them Man when they were created." Even after the fall, Moses insists on the dignity of chapter 1. Fallen people still bear God's likeness, still carry His blessing in their design. A genealogy may look dry, but it is theology in list form: God keeps accounts of ordinary people because ordinary people are how He keeps His promises.

**Adam, Seth, and the image passed on (5:3–5).** Adam fathers Seth "in his own likeness, after his image" — language that echoes God's creation of Adam, with a sobering difference. The image of God is passed on, but now through a fallen father to a fallen son. Seth bears God's likeness and Adam's likeness at once: dignified and dying. Adam lives 930 years — and then the refrain lands for the first time: "and he died." Long life is not eternal life. Nine centuries cannot outrun the sentence of Genesis 2:17. The first man, made directly by God, walked in Eden, and still the record closes the same way it will close for nearly everyone in this chapter.

**The refrain: and he died (5:6–20).** Seth lives 912 years, "and he died." Enosh, 905 years, "and he died." Kenan, Mahalalel, Jared — father, son, grandson, each with sons and daughters, each with centuries most of us cannot imagine, and each entry tolls the same bell. This is Romans 5:12 written as a family record: "sin came into the world through one man, and death through sin, and so death spread to all men because all sinned." Read the chapter aloud and you hear it — death reigns. The names matter, though. God does not record statistics. He records people. In a world that forgets the dead quickly, heaven keeps the book.

**Enoch walked with God (5:21–24).** Then the pattern breaks. "Enoch walked with God after he fathered Methuselah 300 years and had other sons and daughters... Enoch walked with God, and he was not, for God took him." Walking with God is the Bible's picture of a daily, steady, relational life with Him — not a sprint, not a show, but agreement and companionship, step after step, year after year. Enoch was not a hermit; he was a father in the thick of family life for three centuries. And his end is unlike every other: no death recorded. God took him. Hebrews explains that Enoch pleased God and was taken up so that he did not see death (Hebrews 11:5), and Jude adds that Enoch prophesied against the ungodly of his day (Jude 14–15). Walking with God made him both close to heaven and honest on earth. In a chapter full of graves, Enoch is a window open toward resurrection.

**Methuselah and Lamech (5:25–29).** Methuselah lives 969 years, the longest life in the Bible — and he dies, in fact, in the very year of the flood, a standing reminder that even the longest reprieve ends. His son Lamech — not Cain's violent Lamech, but Seth's line — fathers a son and names him Noah, speaking a hope-filled word over the cradle: "Out of the ground that the LORD has cursed, this one shall bring us relief from our work and from the painful toil of our hands." Generations into the curse, the family of faith is still expecting relief. They feel the thorns of Genesis 3 in their own hands, and they look to God to send someone through whom relief will come. That expectation — tired, hopeful, handed down — is exactly what the promise of the Seed looks like in daily life.

**Noah and his sons (5:30–32).** Lamech dies — five years before the flood, spared from seeing it. The chapter closes with Noah at 500 years old, father of Shem, Ham, and Japheth. The genealogy lands where the story needs it: at the man through whom God will preserve the human race and the line of promise through the judgment to come. Ten generations from Adam to Noah, the chain holds. Death took every father, but it could not take the promise. God buries His workers, as the old saying goes, but His work goes on — here, name by name, cradle by grave, until it reaches the flood, and beyond it, Bethlehem.

### Key verses

> "This is the book of the generations of Adam. When God created man, he made him in the likeness of God." — Genesis 5:1 (ESV)

> "Enoch walked with God after he fathered Methuselah 300 years and had other sons and daughters." — Genesis 5:22 (ESV)

> "Enoch walked with God, and he was not, for God took him." — Genesis 5:24 (ESV)

> "and called his name Noah, saying, 'Out of the ground that the LORD has cursed, this one shall bring us relief from our work and from the painful toil of our hands.'" — Genesis 5:29 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news tolls through this chapter like a bell: "and he died" — Adam died, Seth died, every father and mother in the line died, because sin entered through one man and death spread to all, and no length of years, no family, and no good name can exempt any of us from the grave. The good news is that in the middle of the death list God set Enoch, who walked with Him and was taken, not buried — a first sign that death will not have the last word — and God preserved the line itself all the way down to Jesus, who entered our dying family, died our death, and rose again, so that all who walk with God by faith in Him will live even though they die.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin treated this genealogy as a proof of God's providence in preserving the church: through centuries when the world ripened for judgment, God kept a line, a family, and a record, so that the promise would not perish with any single generation. Luther similarly saw in Enoch a comfort for believers — that a life of walking with God in an ungodly age is neither forgotten nor wasted, since the God who took Enoch keeps every one of His own, even when the rest of the page reads "and he died."

**Reflection question:** Enoch simply walked with God, day by day, in an ordinary family life — what would one honest, daily step of walking with God look like for you this week?

## Genesis 6 — Noah and the Ark

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus Himself points us back to Noah. "For as were the days of Noah, so will be the coming of the Son of Man" (Matthew 24:37). In Noah's day people ate, drank, and married, deaf to the warning, until the flood came — and Jesus says His own return will find the world just as unprepared. Noah, then, is a signpost to Christ in two ways: his preaching of righteousness warns us to be ready (2 Peter 2:5), and his ark pictures the salvation Christ provides. There was one ark, one door, and safety only inside it. Jesus says, "I am the door. If anyone enters by me, he will be saved" (John 10:9).

Noah is also a type of Christ in his person. He is called righteous and blameless in his generation, a man who walked with God while the whole earth was corrupt. He stood alone, believed a warning about things not yet seen, and built the means of salvation for his household. In all of that he foreshadows the truly righteous Man who would stand alone in a corrupt age, believe and obey His Father completely, and through His own work provide a refuge for everyone who comes to Him. Peter makes the connection explicit: God preserved Noah, and the water that judged the world in his day points forward to the salvation God gives in Christ (1 Peter 3:20–21).

### Commentary

**Man multiplies, and so does sin (6:1–4).** Mankind multiplies on the face of the earth, and the mysterious account of the "sons of God" and the daughters of man follows, producing the Nephilim, "the mighty men who were of old, the men of renown." Interpreters have long debated whether the sons of God are fallen angels or the godly line of Seth intermarrying with Cain's line; the chapter's main point does not depend on settling it. Boundaries God set are being crossed, violence and fame-seeking fill the earth, and human greatness is measured by power rather than godliness. Renown in man's eyes and righteousness in God's eyes are turning out to be very different things.

**The LORD saw the wickedness (6:5–7).** Now comes one of the most searching verses in Scripture: "The LORD saw that the wickedness of man was great in the earth, and that every intention of the thoughts of his heart was only evil continually."
