Not mostly evil. Not evil in deed only, but in intention, thought, and heart — only evil, continually. This is the Bible's honest diagnosis of fallen man, and it grieves God: "And the LORD regretted that he had made man on the earth, and it grieved him to his heart." This is not God admitting a mistake or learning something new; it is the language of real sorrow — sin genuinely grieves the heart of God. Judgment, when it comes, is not cold. "I will blot out man whom I have created from the face of the land," God says — and the animals with him, for creation itself is tangled in man's fall.

**But Noah found favor (6:8–10).** "But Noah found favor in the eyes of the LORD." That small word "but" carries the whole gospel pattern: judgment deserved by all, grace given to one family for the saving of the world. The word is favor — grace — not wages. Noah is then described as "a righteous man, blameless in his generation," a man who "walked with God," like his great-grandfather Enoch. Grace found Noah, and grace changed Noah: the man who received favor is the man who walks with God and stands blameless while his whole generation goes another way. He fathers Shem, Ham, and Japheth, and through this one household God will carry the human race — and the promised line — through the flood.

**The earth was corrupt and filled with violence (6:11–13).** Twice Moses says the earth was corrupt, and once that it "was filled with violence." The Hebrew picture is of a world ruined and ruining — moral rot expressed in bloodshed, as in Cain's line, now worldwide. God tells Noah plainly, "The end of all flesh has come before me." God's patience has been long — centuries of it, reaching back through Enoch's preaching and Methuselah's long life — but patience is not permission, and the end God announces is just. When God judges, He first says why.

**The ark: God's exact design (6:14–16).** Then God gives Noah the design, and Moses records it in full detail — because the details preach. "Make yourself an ark of gopher wood. Make rooms in the ark, and cover it inside and out with pitch." Gopher wood — a strong, resinous timber — sealed inside and out with pitch, so the vessel that must face judgment water is watertight from every side. The dimensions are exact: "The length of the ark 300 cubits, its breadth 50 cubits, and its height 30 cubits" — roughly 450 feet long, 75 feet wide, and 45 feet high, proportions shipbuilders still recognize as remarkably stable for a great vessel. This is no canoe and no fairy tale; it is a vast, carefully proportioned refuge. God commands a window — "Make a roof for the ark, and finish it to a cubit above" — an opening running around the ark near the top, for light and air, set high so Noah looks up, not out at the storm. "And set the door of the ark in its side" — one door, only one, in the side of the ark. And the inside is ordered: "make it with lower, second, and third decks" — three decks, room upon room, space for Noah's family and for the animals God will bring. Every specification comes from God. Noah does not design his own salvation; he receives the plan and builds it. That is how God has always saved: He designs, He commands, and His people trust and obey.

**The covenant and the creatures (6:17–21).** God announces the flood — "I will bring a flood of waters upon the earth to destroy all flesh" — and in the same breath establishes His covenant with Noah: "But I will establish my covenant with you, and you shall come into the ark, you, your sons, your wife, and your sons' wives with you." This is the first time the word "covenant" appears in the Bible, and it appears as pure promise: God binds Himself to save. Two of every sort of living thing are to come into the ark "to keep them alive with you," and Noah is to gather "every sort of food that is eaten" and store it. Salvation here is not escape for a soul while creation burns — God preserves animals, food, family, and future. He cares about the whole world He made, even while He judges it.

**Noah did all that God commanded (6:22).** The chapter ends with obedience in one sentence: "Noah did this; he did all that God commanded him." No recorded argument, no negotiation, no delay — though the task was enormous and the rain had never been seen. Hebrews says that "by faith Noah, being warned by God concerning events as yet unseen, in reverent fear constructed an ark for the saving of his household" (Hebrews 11:7). Faith is not merely believing God exists. Faith builds what God commands while the sky is still clear and the neighbors are still laughing.

### Key verses

> "The LORD saw that the wickedness of man was great in the earth, and that every intention of the thoughts of his heart was only evil continually." — Genesis 6:5 (ESV)

> "But Noah found favor in the eyes of the LORD." — Genesis 6:8 (ESV)

> "Make yourself an ark of gopher wood. Make rooms in the ark, and cover it inside and out with pitch." — Genesis 6:14 (ESV)

> "But I will establish my covenant with you, and you shall come into the ark, you, your sons, your wife, and your sons' wives with you." — Genesis 6:18 (ESV)

> "Noah did this; he did all that God commanded him." — Genesis 6:22 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is God's own verdict on the fallen heart — every intention of its thoughts only evil continually — and the flood stands forever as proof that God will judge sin, that His patience has a limit, and that no one can build a refuge of his own design sturdy enough to face it. The good news is that before one drop of rain fell, God gave grace to Noah, spoke a covenant promise, and designed the ark Himself — one vessel, one door, room enough for all He would bring in — pointing straight to Jesus Christ, God's own provided refuge, in whom everyone who enters by faith is sealed, safe, and carried through judgment into a new world.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther's Lectures on Genesis dwelt on Noah as a preacher of righteousness who believed and obeyed for over a century in the face of universal scorn — an example, Luther said, that faith clings to God's bare word against all appearances. Calvin emphasized that Noah's righteousness flowed from the favor he first received: God did not find Noah worthy and then show grace; grace made Noah what he was, and Calvin used the exactness of the ark's commands to teach that in matters of salvation we are safe only when we follow God's prescribed way rather than our own inventions.

**Reflection question:** God gave Noah a clear word and a long, costly obedience before any rain fell — what has God already told you to do that you have been putting off until conditions feel easier?

## Genesis 7 — The Flood

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 7 is a chapter of judgment, and Jesus does not hide from it — He preaches from it. "Just as it was in the days of Noah, so will it be in the days of the Son of Man. They were eating and drinking and marrying and being given in marriage, until the day when Noah entered the ark, and the flood came and destroyed them all" (Luke 17:26–27). Jesus treats the flood as real history and as a true preview: God's judgment is certain, it comes in the middle of ordinary life, and the only safety is to be inside the refuge God provided before the door closes.

Peter draws the same line. He says God "did not spare the ancient world, but preserved Noah, a herald of righteousness, with seven others, when he brought a flood upon the world of the ungodly" (2 Peter 2:5) — and Peter's point is comfort for believers: "the Lord knows how to rescue the godly from trials, and to keep the unrighteous under punishment until the day of judgment" (2 Peter 2:9). In the flood we see both halves of Christ's work in shadow: the wrath of God poured out on sin without dilution, and the salvation of God carrying His own safely through the very waters that judged the world. The ark went through the storm, not around it — and so did Jesus, who passed through death itself and brought His people out the other side.

### Commentary

**"Go into the ark" (7:1–3).** God speaks to Noah again: "Go into the ark, you and all your household, for I have seen that you are righteous before me in this generation." After all the building, the time to enter has come, and the command is an invitation as much as an order — God calls the righteous man in. Noah is to take seven pairs of clean animals and of the birds, and one pair of every other kind, "to keep their offspring alive on the face of all the earth." The clean animals matter already: some creatures are being preserved not only for the future of the species but for worship — one of them will be on Noah's altar in the next chapter. God plans for sacrifice and thanksgiving on the far side of judgment before the judgment even begins.

**Seven days of waiting (7:4–5).** "For in seven days I will send rain on the earth forty days and forty nights, and every living thing that I have made I will blot out from the face of the ground." There is a final week of grace — seven days in which Noah enters, the animals are gathered, and the door stands open. Scripture does not record anyone else coming in. Then, simply: "And Noah did all that the LORD had commanded him." Noah's obedience is the steady drumbeat of these chapters. He is six hundred years old, and he is still doing exactly what God says, when God says it. Long obedience in the same direction — that is the shape of a righteous life.

**The entering in (7:6–10).** Noah is 600 years old when the flood comes. Two by two the animals come to him — God brings them; Noah does not have to hunt the earth — and Noah, his sons, his wife, and his sons' wives enter the ark. Eight people in all (1 Peter 3:20). The whole human hope of the world fits inside one household. Moses repeats that they entered "as God had commanded Noah." Safety is not in Noah's cleverness or goodness of heart. It is in being where God said to be, in the vessel God designed, at the time God appointed.

**The fountains and the windows (7:11–12).** The date is given with courtroom precision: "In the six hundredth year of Noah's life, in the second month, on the seventeenth day of the month, on that day all the fountains of the great deep burst forth, and the windows of the heavens were opened. And rain fell upon the earth forty days and forty nights." Water comes from below and from above — the very waters God separated on the second day of creation now rush back together. This is creation being unmade, Genesis 1 run in reverse, because man for whom it was made has filled it with violence. The exact date is Moses' way of saying: this happened, in time, in the real world, and God was in full control of its beginning.

**God shuts the door (7:13–16).** On that very day Noah and his family enter, with every beast, every bird, every creeping thing. Then the chapter's most solemn sentence: "And the LORD shut him in." Noah did not close the door — God did. That shutting is both mercy and judgment in a single act. For those inside, it is perfect security: the hand that sealed the ark shut is the hand that will keep it through everything coming. No wave can open what God has closed. For those outside, it marks the end of the seven days and of every opportunity to enter. There is a door, it stands open by God's grace for a season, and God Himself finally shuts it. Jesus' parables carry the same warning — a shut door, and knocking that comes too late (Matthew 25:10–12). The time to enter is while God is calling.

**The waters prevail (7:17–20).** The flood continues forty days. The waters increase, and notice what they do first: they "bore up the ark, and it rose high above the earth." The same waters that destroy the world lift the ark. Judgment and salvation ride the same flood; the difference is only whether you are inside God's provision or outside it. The waters prevail fifteen cubits above the mountains — over twenty feet above the highest peaks — "and the mountains were covered." There is nowhere to climb to. No height of human achievement, wealth, or respectability rises above this water. When God judges, the only high ground is the refuge He built.

**All flesh died (7:21–23).** The chapter does not flinch: "And all flesh died that moved on the earth, birds, livestock, beasts, all swarming creatures that swarm on the earth, and all mankind." Everything in whose nostrils was the breath of life died. "Only Noah was left, and those who were with him in the ark." The sentence is devastating and deliberate. Sin's wages, announced in Eden, are paid on a worldwide scale. Yet even here, read carefully: God blots out, and God preserves. The same chapter that records the death of a world records eight people safe in a vessel of gopher wood and pitch, because God established a covenant and kept it.

**One hundred and fifty days (7:24).** "And the waters prevailed on the earth 150 days." Five months of water over everything, with a wooden ark riding on top of a drowned world, and no word from heaven recorded. Noah's faith is not only for the building or the entering. It is for the long middle — the waiting inside the dark vessel with nothing to do but trust that the God who shut him in has not forgotten him. Many believers know that season. The chapter ends in silence and water. It will take the first verse of chapter 8 to break it — and the words that break it are worth the wait.

### Key verses

> "Then the LORD said to Noah, 'Go into the ark, you and all your household, for I have seen that you are righteous before me in this generation.'" — Genesis 7:1 (ESV)

> "And those that entered, male and female of all flesh, went in as God had commanded him. And the LORD shut him in." — Genesis 7:16 (ESV)

> "He blotted out every living thing that was on the face of the ground, man and animals and creeping things and birds of the heavens. They were blotted out from the earth. Only Noah was left, and those who were with him in the ark." — Genesis 7:23 (ESV)

> "And the waters prevailed on the earth 150 days." — Genesis 7:24 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that the flood is real judgment: God will not wink at sin forever, His patience runs out, the door He opens He also shuts, and on that day no mountain of human pride stands above the water — every one of us deserves to be outside that ark, blotted out with a world that filled itself with violence. The good news is that God Himself provided the ark, called a household into it, and shut them in with His own hand, and that same God has provided Jesus Christ, who bore the flood of God's wrath at the cross and rose above it, so that everyone who is in Him — hidden in Him, sealed by His Spirit — is carried safely through judgment and will never be shut out.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, on the flood, insisted that the universality of the judgment teaches the universality of human corruption — and that Noah's preservation teaches the church in every age that God knows His own, keeps them by His own power in the midst of a perishing world, and never leaves His covenant to chance. Spurgeon loved the shut door as a text for both sides of the gospel: the believer's complete security — shut in by God Himself, no leak, no fear — and the urgent call to enter now, while the door of mercy in Christ still stands open, before it is shut forever.

**Reflection question:** Are you in the ark — truly trusting in Christ alone — or still standing outside, meaning to enter someday? What is keeping you from coming in today, while the door is open?

## Genesis 8 — The Waters Recede

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 8 opens with the words Noah had waited five months to live inside: "But God remembered Noah." In Scripture, God's remembering is never God recalling something He forgot. It is God acting on His covenant promise at the appointed time. The same word is used when God remembers Rachel, when He remembers His covenant with Abraham in Egypt — and it reaches its height when God remembers His mercy in Christ, sending Jesus at exactly the time He had promised, for people who could only wait and trust.

The chapter also gives us one of the clearest Old Testament pictures of Christ's sacrifice. Noah's first act on dry ground is to build an altar and offer burnt offerings, and God smells the "pleasing aroma" and promises never again to curse the ground or strike down every living creature. Paul uses that exact language of Christ: He "gave himself up for us, a fragrant offering and sacrifice to God" (Ephesians 5:2). Noah's altar points to the cross, where a greater offering turned away wrath and secured God's promise for the world. And the whole chapter breathes new creation: a wind — the Spirit-word again — passes over the waters as in Genesis 1, a dove hovers and returns with an olive leaf, and God blesses Noah with the very words spoken to Adam: "Be fruitful and multiply." In Christ, Paul says, that new creation has already begun: "If anyone is in Christ, he is a new creation" (2 Corinthians 5:17).

### Commentary

**God remembered Noah (8:1).** After 150 days of prevailing water, the turn comes: "But God remembered Noah and all the beasts and all the livestock that were with him in the ark. And God made a wind blow over the earth, and the waters subsided." God remembered the animals too — His covenant care extends to every creature He carried through. And the wind blowing over the earth echoes Genesis 1:2, where the Spirit hovered over the waters. The same God, by the same Spirit, is beginning the world again. If you are in a long season of waiting — in the ark, in the dark, with no visible change — this verse is for you. Silence is not forgetfulness. God's calendar and ours are different, but His memory never fails.

**The waters recede (8:2–5).** The fountains of the deep and the windows of the heavens are stopped, the rain is restrained, and the waters recede from the earth steadily — gradually, not all at once. At the end of 150 days the waters had abated, and the ark comes to rest "on the mountains of Ararat." God could have dried the earth in a day. Instead He works steadily, over months. That is how He usually delivers His people: not always by instant miracle, but by a faithful, measurable recession of the waters, until one day the vessel of your life rests on solid ground again. The ark rests before Noah ever steps out — God settles His people before He sends them.

**The raven and the dove (8:6–12).** After forty days Noah opens the window of the ark — the window God designed near the roof — and sends out a raven, which flies back and forth over the water, content to feed on a drowned world. Then he sends a dove, which finds no place to set her foot and returns to the ark, and Noah puts out his hand and takes her in. Seven days later he sends her again, and this time she returns "and behold, in her mouth a freshly plucked olive leaf" — life is rising out of judgment; the world is green again. Seven more days, and the dove does not return: dry land is ready. The dove has become one of Scripture's enduring signs — of the Spirit hovering over new creation, of peace after wrath, of the olive branch of reconciliation. Noah learns patience in sevens: send, wait, receive, wait again. He does not rush out of the ark on the strength of one olive leaf. He waits for God's word.

**Noah waits for the command (8:13–17).** By the first day of the new year, the waters are dried from the earth, and Noah removes the covering of the ark and sees the ground is dry. Yet he still does not leave. Weeks pass — until the twenty-seventh day of the second month — and only then does God speak: "Go out from the ark, you and your wife, and your sons and your sons' wives with you. Bring out with you every living thing that is with you of all flesh... that they may swarm on the earth, and be fruitful and multiply on the earth." Noah waited a full year inside the ark, and he would not trade God's timing for his own impatience even at the very end. Getting into the ark took faith. Getting out of it took the same faith. The man who entered at God's word would only exit at God's word.

**Out into a new world (8:18–19).** So Noah goes out, with his sons, his wife, and his sons' wives — the whole household that entered together comes out together; God lost none of them. And every beast, every creeping thing, and every bird goes out "by families" — orderly, preserved, ready to fill the earth again. Stand back and see it: eight people walk out into an empty, washed world, the only family on earth, carrying the whole future of mankind — and of the promised Seed — with them. Everything now depends on God's covenant faithfulness, which is exactly where God wants His people: dependent on grace, in a world made new, with everything ahead.

**The altar and the pleasing aroma (8:20–22).** Noah's first work in the new world is not building a house. It is building an altar. "Then Noah built an altar to the LORD and took some of every clean animal and some of every clean bird and offered burnt offerings on the altar." Worship comes first — gratitude before rebuilding. Some of those precious clean animals, preserved by sevens for this very purpose, go up in smoke to God. And God responds from the heart: "And when the LORD smelled the pleasing aroma, the LORD said in his heart, 'I will never again curse the ground because of man, for the intention of man's heart is evil from his youth. Neither will I ever again strike down every living creature as I have done. While the earth remains, seedtime and harvest, cold and heat, summer and winter, day and night, shall not cease.'" Look closely at God's reasoning: He promises mercy not because man's heart has improved — He says plainly it has not — but because of the sacrifice. Grace rests on the offering, not on the improvement of man. And the promise is sweeping: as long as earth remains, the seasons will keep their round. Every sunrise, every harvest, every ordinary Tuesday is this promise being kept — God's covenant patience toward a world that still does not deserve it, holding back judgment and holding open the door of the gospel until Christ returns.

### Key verses

> "But God remembered Noah and all the beasts and all the livestock that were with him in the ark. And God made a wind blow over the earth, and the waters subsided." — Genesis 8:1 (ESV)

> "Then Noah built an altar to the LORD and took some of every clean animal and some of every clean bird and offered burnt offerings on the altar." — Genesis 8:20 (ESV)

> "And when the LORD smelled the pleasing aroma, the LORD said in his heart, 'I will never again curse the ground because of man, for the intention of man's heart is evil from his youth. Neither will I ever again strike down every living creature as I have done.'" — Genesis 8:21 (ESV)

> "While the earth remains, seedtime and harvest, cold and heat, summer and winter, day and night, shall not cease." — Genesis 8:22 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news stands right inside God's own promise: the flood did not wash the human heart clean — "the intention of man's heart is evil from his youth" — so judgment water alone can never save us, and left to ourselves we would fill a new world with the same old sin and deserve the same end. The good news is that God remembered Noah, received his sacrifice with pleasure, and bound Himself by covenant never again to destroy the earth this way — and in Jesus Christ, the greater sacrifice whose offering truly pleased the Father, God has gone further still: He gives new hearts, not just a new world, remembering His people in mercy, drying their floodwaters steadily, and keeping every season of this earth turning until He brings them into the final new creation where the waters of judgment will never rise again.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther found deep comfort in "God remembered Noah," preaching that God's remembering is His covenant faithfulness in action — that He seems to forget His people for a season only that His deliverance, when it comes, shows itself to be pure grace. Calvin, on the altar and the promise, stressed that God's resolve never to destroy the earth again rests on the sacrifice, not on any change in man, and that the unbroken round of seedtime and harvest is a daily, public proof of God's fatherly patience toward the whole human race.

**Reflection question:** Noah's first act in the new world was an altar, not a house — what act of worship or gratitude should come first in your life as the waters recede, before all the rebuilding?

## Genesis 9 — The Covenant with Noah

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is not named in Genesis 9, and there is no clear appearance of Christ here, so we should say that plainly and not force one. What we do find is the covenant framework that Christ will one day fulfill. When God sets His bow in the cloud and promises never again to destroy the earth by flood, He is preserving a world — and a human family — through which the promised Seed will come. Every generation that lives on after Noah lives because God kept this promise. The line that will lead to Abraham, to David, and to Jesus survives here, under this bow.

We also see the pattern of Christ's work in the covering of Noah's shame. When Noah lies exposed in his tent, Shem and Japheth walk backward and cover their father without looking on his nakedness. It is a small, quiet act, but it points forward to a greater covering. In Christ, God covers the shame of sinners — not by pretending the shame is not there, but by providing a covering Himself, at cost to Himself. The blood of Jesus does what a garment laid over a sleeping man could only picture.

### Commentary

**A new beginning that sounds like Eden (9:1–7).** God blesses Noah and his sons with the same words He spoke over Adam: "Be fruitful and multiply and fill the earth." This is a second beginning for mankind. The animals will now fear man in a new way, and for the first time God gives meat for food, as He had already given the green plants. But there is a limit, and the limit matters: "But you shall not eat flesh with its life, that is, its blood." Life belongs to God. Blood stands for life, and life is not ours to treat carelessly. That single command will echo through the rest of Scripture, all the way to the law of Moses, and it prepares us to understand why blood matters so much when God makes atonement for sin. God also guards human life itself: "Whoever sheds the blood of man, by man shall his blood be shed, for God made man in his own image." Even after the fall, even after the flood, man still bears God's image. That is why murder is not just a crime against a person — it is an offense against God, whose image that person carries. For anyone who has been told their life is worthless, hear this chapter carefully: God built the worth of your life into creation itself, and He put a fence around it.

**The covenant and the bow (9:8–17).** Now God makes a covenant — not only with Noah, but with his sons, with their descendants, and "with every living creature." This covenant is pure gift. Noah does not bargain for it, earn it, or even speak in this section. God speaks, God promises, God gives the sign. The promise is that never again will a flood cut off all flesh and destroy the earth. The sign is "my bow in the cloud" — the rainbow. Think about what a bow is. A bow is a weapon of war. Here God hangs His bow in the sky, pointed away from the earth, retired from use against us in this way. And notice who the sign is mainly for: "When I bring clouds over the earth and the bow is seen in the clouds, I will remember my covenant." God says *He* will remember. The rainbow is not first a reminder for us to be good. It is God's own sign to Himself of His own promise — and then, second, a comfort for us every time we see it. After a storm, when the clouds are still dark, the bow appears. That is exactly when a frightened heart needs it.

**Noah's shame and his sons (9:18–23).** The chapter does not hide what happens next, and we should not hide it either. Noah plants a vineyard, drinks of the wine, becomes drunk, and lies uncovered in his tent. The man who found grace in the eyes of the Lord, who walked with God and built the ark, falls into shame in his own home. Scripture is honest about its heroes because it wants us to put our hope in God, not in men. Ham sees his father's nakedness and tells his brothers — he exposes the shame and spreads it. Shem and Japheth do the opposite. They take a garment, walk in backward, and cover their father, their faces turned away. There is a way to deal with another person's failure that covers and restores, and a way that exposes and enjoys it. This little scene still divides people today.

**The prophecy over the sons (9:24–29).** When Noah awakes, he speaks words that reach far beyond his tent: "Cursed be Canaan; a servant of servants shall he be to his brothers." Canaan is Ham's son, and his descendants will become the peoples of Canaan, whose wickedness will one day fill up and bring judgment when Israel enters the land. "Blessed be the LORD, the God of Shem" — notice, Noah blesses not Shem himself first, but Shem's God. The promise line runs through Shem. From Shem will come Abraham, and from Abraham, in time, the Christ. Japheth is promised enlargement and a share in Shem's tents — a picture many have seen fulfilled as Gentile nations later come in and dwell in the blessing that came through Shem's line, through Israel, through Jesus. Noah lives 350 years after the flood, 950 years in all, and then he dies. The refrain of Genesis 5 returns: even the righteous die. The problem of death is not solved yet. We are still waiting for the Seed.

### Key verses

> "Whoever sheds the blood of man, by man shall his blood be shed, for God made man in his own image." — Genesis 9:6 (ESV)

> "I establish my covenant with you, that never again shall all flesh be cut off by the waters of the flood, and never again shall there be a flood to destroy the earth." — Genesis 9:11 (ESV)

> "I have set my bow in the cloud, and it shall be a sign of the covenant between me and the earth." — Genesis 9:13 (ESV)

> "When the bow is in the clouds, I will see it and remember the everlasting covenant between God and every living creature of all flesh that is on the earth." — Genesis 9:16 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that the flood did not wash sin out of the human heart — Noah himself, fresh off the ark, ends up drunk and shamed in his own tent, and his family fractures around his failure, which means no fresh start, no new program, and no strong man can fix what is broken in us. But that bad news points to the good news: God did not rest the future on Noah's strength — He rested it on His own covenant promise, hung His bow in the clouds, and kept the world and the promise line alive until He Himself provided the covering for our shame in Jesus Christ, whose blood does what Noah's sons' garment could only picture.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, in his commentary on Genesis, draws comfort from the fact that the covenant here is made even with the beasts and rests entirely on God's promise rather than on man's worthiness — Noah's fall, coming so soon after, proves the promise could never have stood on human ground. Luther likewise stresses in his Lectures on Genesis that the rainbow is a sign of grace given to trembling consciences: God binds Himself, so that after every storm the believer has something outside himself to look at and trust.

**Reflection question:** When the clouds gather in your life, do you look at your own record — or at the sign God Himself hung in the sky to say He remembers His promise?

## Genesis 10 — The Table of Nations

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 10 reads, at first glance, like a list of names — but it is a map of the world Jesus came to save. "In you all the families of the earth shall be blessed," God will say to Abraham in the very next movement of Genesis, and this chapter shows us those families. Every nation named here — from the coastlands of Japheth to the cities of Ham to the clans of Shem — is made up of people bearing God's image, scattered across the earth, and included in the wide scope of God's saving purpose. When Jesus sends His disciples to "all nations" in Matthew 28, He is sending them to the families of Genesis 10.

More narrowly, the chapter quietly marks the one line through which Christ will come. Among all these nations, the line of Shem is singled out at the end: "To Shem also, the father of all the children of Eber, the elder brother of Japheth, children were born." From Shem comes Eber, from Eber comes the people called Hebrews, and down that line — named again in chapter 11 — runs the road to Bethlehem. The chapter holds both truths at once: God's heart is for all the nations, and His Son comes through one particular family.

### Commentary

**The sons of Japheth (10:1–5).** The chapter opens with its purpose stated plainly: "These are the generations of the sons of Noah, Shem, Ham, and Japheth. Sons were born to them after the flood." Japheth's line is listed first, though he is not the line of promise. His descendants — Gomer, Magog, Javan, Tubal, and the rest — spread toward the coastlands and islands of the Mediterranean and beyond: "From these the coastland peoples spread in their lands, each with his own language, by their clans, in their nations." That closing phrase will repeat like a drumbeat through the chapter. Lands, languages, clans, nations. God is not the God of one tribe only. He is filling the earth, exactly as He commanded, and He keeps track of every people. Some of these names will reappear centuries later in the prophets — Magog, Tubal, Javan — sometimes as distant powers, sometimes as enemies. But they are never outside God's knowledge or His rule.

**The sons of Ham (10:6–20).** Ham's line runs toward Egypt, Cush, and Canaan. Here we meet Nimrod, one of the few men in the chapter given more than a name: "He was the first on earth to be a mighty man. He was a mighty hunter before the LORD." Nimrod is a kingdom-builder — Babel and Nineveh are both tied to his name — and he stands at the head of a pattern we will see again and again: human might building cities and empires apart from trust in God. Babel, which dominates the next chapter, is planted here in a single verse. Egypt, called Mizraim here, will become the house of slavery for God's people — and also, centuries later, the place that sheltered the infant Jesus when Herod hunted Him. And Canaan's line is listed in careful detail, border by border, including Sodom and Gomorrah and the peoples Israel will later face. The detail is there for a reason: the land promised to Abraham is not empty or unknown. It is inhabited by named peoples with named borders, and God knows exactly what He is promising.

**The sons of Shem (10:21–31).** Shem's list is saved for last, and it is the list that matters most for the story of redemption. Shem is identified as "the father of all the children of Eber" — Eber is the name behind the word "Hebrew," the name Abraham will carry. The line runs Shem, Arpachshad, Shelah, Eber — and then Eber's two sons divide the story: Peleg, "for in his days the earth was divided," a quiet pointer to Babel, and Joktan, whose sons spread eastward and fade from the story. Peleg's line is the one to watch. Chapter 11 will pick it up name by name and walk it straight to Abram. Nothing in this section sparkles. There are no miracles, no speeches. There is just a chain of fathers and sons — and God is in the chain, carrying His promise from one generation to the next, often through people history would otherwise forget.

**Seventy nations and one world under God (10:32).** The chapter closes: "These are the clans of the sons of Noah, according to their genealogies, in their nations, and from these the nations spread abroad on the earth after the flood." By the traditional count, seventy nations are named — a number that comes to stand for the whole of mankind. That is the point to carry away. All nations come from one family. There is no people on earth that did not come through the ark, no race or nation closer to God by birth than another, and none beyond His reach. When Israel later offers seventy bulls at the Feast of Booths, many see an echo of this table — sacrifices for all the nations. And when the Holy Spirit falls at Pentecost and men from nation after nation hear the gospel in their own tongues, Genesis 10 is the backdrop: the families of the earth, gathered at last under the blessing.

### Key verses

> "These are the generations of the sons of Noah, Shem, Ham, and Japheth. Sons were born to them after the flood." — Genesis 10:1 (ESV)

> "From these the coastland peoples spread in their lands, each with his own language, by their clans, in their nations." — Genesis 10:5 (ESV)

> "To Shem also, the father of all the children of Eber, the elder brother of Japheth, children were born." — Genesis 10:21 (ESV)

> "These are the clans of the sons of Noah, according to their genealogies, in their nations, and from these the nations spread abroad on the earth after the flood." — Genesis 10:32 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that this table of nations is a family divided — one human race, descended from one boatload of survivors, already spreading out into separate lands and languages that will spend history at war with one another, and even Nimrod's might in this chapter is the seed of Babel's rebellion — yet the bad news points to the good news: God never lost track of a single nation on this list, He kept Shem's line alive through it all, and through that line He sent Jesus, in whom the scattered families of the earth are being gathered into one people again, from every tribe and language under heaven.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin treats this chapter as a mirror of God's providence, noting that Moses records the nations' spread so believers would know that the whole world, however divided, remains under God's government and care — no people has wandered beyond His hand. Luther, lecturing on these genealogies, marvels at how God preserves His church hidden inside the mass of the nations: the line of promise runs quietly through an ordinary list of names, unseen by the world, but never lost to God.

**Reflection question:** When you think of people far from God — other neighborhoods, other nations, people nothing like you — does Genesis 10 change how you see them: one family, known by name to God, and meant for the blessing?

## Genesis 11 — Babel and the Line of Shem

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus stands at the far end of this chapter in two directions at once. First, He is the answer to Babel. At Babel, mankind tried to climb to heaven on its own bricks and make a name for itself; God scattered them in judgment. In Christ, God comes down — as He came down at Babel, but this time in mercy — and gathers the scattered. Pentecost is Babel reversed: instead of one language confused into many, the many languages hear one gospel, and people from every nation are gathered into one body under one Name. The name men tried and failed to make for themselves at Babel, God gives freely in Jesus: a name that lasts, belonging to all who believe.

Second, Jesus is the end of the genealogy that closes the chapter. The dry list of names from Shem to Abram is not filler — it is the narrowing road of promise, generation by generation, until it arrives at the family from which Christ will come. Luke will trace Jesus' own family line back through this very territory. Every "and he died" in the list is a reminder that these men could not save themselves; the line survives because God keeps it, until it reaches the One who does not die.

### Commentary

**One language, one proud plan (11:1–4).** "Now the whole earth had one language and the same words." Mankind migrates east and settles on a plain in Shinar — and settles is the key word. God had commanded them to fill the earth; they decide instead to cluster and stay. With bricks and bitumen — man-made stone and man-made mortar — they launch their project: "Come, let us build ourselves a city and a tower with its top in the heavens, and let us make a name for ourselves, lest we be dispersed over the face of the whole earth." Read their motive carefully. It is stated in plain words: *a name for ourselves*, and *lest we be dispersed* — the exact opposite of God's command and God's promise. God had promised to make a name for Noah's line through Shem; these builders want to seize a name for themselves. This is the same old sin in new clothes: Eden's "you will be like God," built now with bricks. Every addiction, every proud scheme, every life built on "I will make my own name and secure myself" is Babel under construction.

**The LORD comes down (11:5–9).** The irony is sharp and deliberate: the tower meant to reach the heavens is so small that "the LORD came down to see the city and the tower." God is not threatened. He inspects. And His judgment fits the sin with precision. They feared being scattered, so He scatters them. They prized their one language, so He confuses it: "Come, let us go down and there confuse their language, so that they may not understand one another's speech." The name Babel sounds like the Hebrew word for confusion, and Moses wants us to hear it: the city of man's great name becomes the city of man's great muddle. Yet even this judgment carries mercy inside it. A united mankind, united in rebellion, would only grow worse — God scatters them as a restraint, the way a father breaks up a gang headed for destruction. And scattered, they finally do what God commanded all along: they fill the earth.

**The line of Shem narrows (11:10–26).** After the wide shot of the nations, Moses zooms in: "These are the generations of Shem." The names march down — Arpachshad, Shelah, Eber, Peleg, Reu, Serug, Nahor, Terah — and something sobering marches with them: the lifespans shrink. Shem lives 600 years; by Terah's day, 205 is a long life. Sin's wages are working their way through the race. Each entry ends, in effect, with the same quiet fact: sons and daughters were born, years passed, and the man died. But the line does not die. That is the whole point. God is carrying something through these ordinary, shrinking lives — and nobody in the list, except perhaps Shem who saw the flood, has any idea what it is. If your faithfulness feels small and unnoticed, this genealogy is for you. God does some of His greatest work through generations nobody remembers, simply keeping His promise alive one family at a time.

**Terah's family and the move that stalled (11:27–32).** The chapter ends by introducing the family everything will now follow. Terah fathers Abram, Nahor, and Haran. Haran dies young, "in the presence of his father," leaving a son named Lot. Abram's wife Sarai is introduced with a painful detail slipped into the record without explanation: "Sarai was barren; she had no child." Hold onto that sentence. The promise of a great nation is about to be given to a man whose wife cannot have children. Then Terah does something remarkable: he takes Abram, Lot, and Sarai and sets out "from Ur of the Chaldeans to go into the land of Canaan." He aimed for Canaan — but "when they came to Haran, they settled there." He stopped halfway, in a town bearing his dead son's name, and there he died, 205 years old. How many lives stall at Haran — started toward God's call, settled short of it, comfortable in the halfway place? Genesis leaves Terah there, and turns its face toward Abram, still in Haran, waiting for a voice.

### Key verses

> "Then they said, 'Come, let us build ourselves a city and a tower with its top in the heavens, and let us make a name for ourselves, lest we be dispersed over the face of the whole earth.'" — Genesis 11:4 (ESV)

> "Therefore its name was called Babel, because there the LORD confused the language of all the earth. And from there the LORD dispersed them over the face of all the earth." — Genesis 11:9 (ESV)

> "Now Sarai was barren; she had no child." — Genesis 11:30 (ESV)

> "Terah took Abram his son and Lot the son of Haran, his grandson, and Sarai his daughter-in-law, his son Abram's wife, and they went forth together from Ur of the Chaldeans to go into the land of Canaan, but when they came to Haran, they settled there." — Genesis 11:31 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that left to ourselves we all build Babel — we try to make our own name, secure our own future, and climb to God on our own bricks, and the end of it is confusion, scattering, and lives that stall halfway at Haran — but the bad news points to the good news: God came down, and He did not stop coming down at Babel; He kept a single family line alive through shrinking, dying generations, and in the fullness of time He came down in Jesus Christ, who gives sinners His own name freely and gathers the scattered peoples of the earth into one family by grace.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther, in his Lectures on Genesis, treats Babel as the founding picture of all self-righteous religion: men building a tower of their own works to reach heaven, which God in mercy frustrates. Calvin makes a similar point from the other side, observing that God's scattering was both judgment and kindness — a restraint on united wickedness — and that the church, by contrast, is gathered not by human building but by God's Word in every tongue.

**Reflection question:** Where have you settled at your own Haran — started toward what God called you to, but stopped halfway — and what would it take to hear His voice and move again?


## Genesis 12 — The Call of Abram

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 12:3 is one of the most important verses in the whole Bible for seeing Jesus: "in you all the families of the earth shall be blessed." Paul, in Galatians 3, reads that promise and tells us plainly what it means — the blessing that comes to the nations through Abraham's offspring is the gospel itself, and the offspring is Christ. Jesus is the Seed of Abram through whom blessing — forgiveness, righteousness, life with God — flows out to every family on earth. When you became a believer, whoever you are and wherever you come from, you stepped into a promise God made to an old man in Haran thousands of years before you were born.

Jesus is also foreshadowed here by contrast. Abram is called to leave his country, his kindred, and his father's house for a land he has never seen, trusting God's word alone. Christ did the greater leaving: He left His Father's house in heaven, came to a land that would reject Him, and did it not for His own blessing but for ours. And where Abram fails in this very chapter — in Egypt, out of fear — Christ never fails. The story needs a better Abraham, and God will provide Him from Abraham's own line.

### Commentary

**The call and the great promise (12:1–3).** The chapter opens with God's voice breaking into Abram's stalled life in Haran: "Go from your country and your kindred and your father's house to the land that I will show you." Notice what God does not give him: no map, no timetable, no name for the destination. Just a command and a promise. And what a promise it is. "And I will make of you a great nation, and I will bless you and make your name great, so that you will be a blessing." At Babel, men tried to make their own name great and were scattered. Here, God *gives* a great name as a gift — that is the difference between Babel and faith, between grasping and receiving. "I will bless those who bless you, and him who dishonors you I will curse" — God binds His own honor to this childless old man. Then the promise widens to its widest: "and in you all the families of the earth shall be blessed." All the families — the seventy nations of Genesis 10, scattered at Babel in Genesis 11 — will be blessed through this one man's offspring. God's answer to the scattering of the nations is not a tower. It is a family, and a promise, and eventually a Savior.

**Abram obeys and worships (12:4–9).** "So Abram went, as the LORD had told him." Five plain words, and a lifetime inside them. He is seventy-five years old. He takes Sarai, Lot, their possessions, and the people they had acquired, and sets out for Canaan — finishing the journey his father Terah started and abandoned. When he arrives, the Canaanites are in the land; the promise is not empty real estate waiting politely. Then the LORD appears to him — the first recorded appearance of God to Abram in Canaan — and narrows the promise: "To your offspring I will give this land." Abram's response is worship. He builds an altar at Shechem, then another between Bethel and Ai, and there he "called upon the name of the LORD." Altars and calling on God's name: that is the shape of Abram's life from here on. He is a pilgrim who owns nothing of the land yet except the places where he worships. If you are following God into territory you do not yet possess, that is not a sign you took a wrong turn. That is what the life of faith has always looked like.

**Failure in Egypt (12:10–20).** Then comes famine — and the chapter takes an honest, ugly turn. Abram goes down to Egypt to survive, and on the way he talks Sarai into a half-lie to protect his own skin: "Say you are my sister, that it may go well with me because of you, and that my life may be spared for your sake." She was his half-sister, but the intent was deception, and the motive was fear. It works — for Abram. Pharaoh takes Sarai into his house, showers Abram with livestock and servants, and Abram grows rich while his wife sits in another man's household. The father of the faithful, in this chapter, is a coward who endangered his wife to save himself. But watch God. God afflicts Pharaoh's house with plagues for Sarai's sake. God protects the promise when Abram will not — protects the woman through whom the promised offspring must come, rebukes Abram through a pagan king's mouth ("What is this you have done to me?"), and sends him out of Egypt with everything intact. This is not in the Bible to excuse Abram. It is here to show you what kind of God makes promises: the kind who keeps them even when His people are the biggest threat to them. Many of us have an Egypt chapter — a season where fear ran our mouth and we compromised people we love. Genesis 12 says that chapter is real, it is shameful, and it is not the end of the story, because the promise rests on God.

### Key verses

> "Now the LORD said to Abram, 'Go from your country and your kindred and your father's house to the land that I will show you.'" — Genesis 12:1 (ESV)

> "And I will make of you a great nation, and I will bless you and make your name great, so that you will be a blessing." — Genesis 12:2 (ESV)

> "I will bless those who bless you, and him who dishonors you I will curse, and in you all the families of the earth shall be blessed." — Genesis 12:3 (ESV)

> "Then the LORD appeared to Abram and said, 'To your offspring I will give this land.' So he built there an altar to the LORD, who had appeared to him." — Genesis 12:7 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that even a man called by God, blessed by God, and promised the world by God will, in a famine, run on fear and lie to protect himself at the expense of the wife he loves — faith and failure live in the same man, and our compromises endanger the very people our calling was meant to bless — but the bad news points straight to the good news: the promise never rested on Abram's courage; God Himself guarded Sarai, guarded the promise, and carried it forward to the true Seed of Abraham, Jesus Christ, in whom all the families of the earth — including families wrecked by fear and failure — are blessed.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, on this chapter, stresses that Abram's faith was obedience to a bare word — he left everything for a land described only as "the land that I will show you," which Calvin holds up as the pattern of all faith: trusting God's promise when sight gives us nothing. Luther, in his Lectures on Genesis, is blunt about the Egypt failure and just as strong on the comfort of it: Abraham's sin is recorded so that believers who fall will not despair, since God's promise stood firm even while Abraham's faith shook.

**Reflection question:** What has God called you to leave behind — a place, a pattern, a fear-driven way of protecting yourself — and are you trusting His bare promise enough to go?

## Genesis 13 — Abram and Lot Separate

### Jesus in this chapter

In Genesis 13 we see Christ by way of a pattern and a promise. The pattern is Abram's peacemaking. When strife breaks out between his herdsmen and Lot's, Abram — the elder, the one who received the promise, the one with every right to choose first — gives the choice away: "Let there be no strife between you and me... Is not the whole land before you?" He surrenders his rights for the sake of peace. That is the shape of Christ, who, though He was in the form of God, did not grasp at His rights but emptied Himself and made peace by the blood of His cross. Abram's generosity here is a shadow; Jesus is the substance.

The promise is the land itself, renewed to Abram right after he lets it go: "for all the land that you see I will give to you and to your offspring forever." The New Testament reads this promise bigger than Canaan's borders. Paul says in Romans 4 that the promise to Abraham was that he would be heir of *the world*. The land Abram walked through is a first installment — the down payment on the new heavens and new earth that Christ, Abraham's Seed, will inherit and share with all who believe.

### Commentary

**Too blessed to stay together (13:1–7).** Abram comes up out of Egypt "very rich in livestock, in silver, and in gold" — rich, ironically, partly through the gifts of the Pharaoh episode he should never have been in. Grace is like that; God blesses us out of chapters we are ashamed of. Lot, too, has flocks and herds and tents. The land cannot support them together, and strife breaks out between their herdsmen. Moses adds a pointed detail: "At that time the Canaanites and the Perizzites were dwelling in the land." The watching world is present for this family fight. Unbelieving neighbors have always watched believers quarrel, and the quarrel itself becomes the testimony. Before a word is said about who gets what pasture, the real issue is on the table: the people of promise are fighting each other in front of the very nations they were called to bless.

**Abram gives away his right (13:8–9).** Abram's response is one of the finest moments in his life. He goes to Lot — his nephew, his junior, the one who owed him everything — and he speaks first, and he speaks peace: "Let there be no strife between you and me, and between your herdsmen and my herdsmen, for we are kinsmen. Is not the whole land before you? Separate yourself from me. If you take the left hand, then I will go to the right, or if you take the right hand, then I will go to the left." Everything about this is upside down by the world's reckoning. The elder defers to the younger. The promise-bearer offers the choice to the one with no promise. Abram can afford to be generous because his future does not hang on pasture — it hangs on God's word. That is the secret of a non-grasping life. People who are sure God will keep His promise do not have to fight for every advantage. People who are not sure grab, scheme, and strife follows. Abram, fresh from learning in Egypt that God guards the promise even when he fails, now rests in it — and resting, he can open his hands.

**Lot chooses with his eyes (13:10–13).** Lot looks, and chooses by sight alone. The Jordan Valley is "well watered everywhere... like the garden of the LORD, like the land of Egypt." It is beautiful, fertile, and prosperous — and Moses slips in the warning twice. The valley is lush "before the LORD destroyed Sodom and Gomorrah," and Lot pitches his tents "as far as Sodom," whose men "were wicked, great sinners against the LORD." Lot chose the best-looking land nearest the worst place on earth, and he chose it in stages — first the valley, then near Sodom, and by chapter 14 he is living *in* Sodom, and by chapter 19 he is sitting in its gate, having lost nearly everything. Nobody drifts into Sodom in one day. Lot's choice looked smart by every earthly measure — water, grass, profit — and he never once appears to have asked what it would do to his soul or his family. It is the Bible's standing warning against choosing by eyes alone: the greenest pasture can border the darkest city.

**The promise renewed to Abram's eyes and feet (13:14–18).** Only after Lot separates does God speak — and note the timing. Abram gave the land away, and *then* God gives it all to him: "Lift up your eyes and look from the place where you are, northward and southward and eastward and westward, for all the land that you see I will give to you and to your offspring forever." Lot lifted up his eyes and grabbed what he saw. God now tells Abram to lift up his eyes and *receive* what he sees. Same eyes, opposite postures — one grasping, one receiving. The promise grows, too: offspring "as the dust of the earth," beyond counting. Then God puts feet to it: "Arise, walk through the length and the breadth of the land, for I will give it to you." In the ancient world, walking a piece of land was a way of taking possession of it. Abram walks a land he does not yet own, by faith, as if it were already his — because in God's promise, it is. He settles by the oaks of Mamre at Hebron, and what does he build? Another altar. Possession by promise, marked by worship.

### Key verses

> "Let there be no strife between you and me, and between your herdsmen and my herdsmen, for we are kinsmen." — Genesis 13:8 (ESV)

> "And Lot lifted up his eyes and saw that the Jordan Valley was well watered everywhere like the garden of the LORD, like the land of Egypt, in the direction of Zoar. (This was before the LORD destroyed Sodom and Gomorrah.)" — Genesis 13:10 (ESV)

> "The LORD said to Abram, after Lot had separated from him, 'Lift up your eyes and look from the place where you are, northward and southward and eastward and westward, for all the land that you see I will give to you and to your offspring forever.'" — Genesis 13:14–15 (ESV)

> "I will make your offspring as the dust of the earth, so that if one can count the dust of the earth, your offspring also can be counted." — Genesis 13:16 (ESV)

> "Arise, walk through the length and the breadth of the land, for I will give it to you." — Genesis 13:17 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that we are all natural Lots — we choose by what looks good to our eyes, drift toward Sodom one reasonable step at a time, and call it wisdom while it costs us our families and our souls; and even believers, blessed richly, fall to fighting each other over pasture while the world watches — but the bad news points to the good news: God meets the man who opens his hands, renews the promise to the one who surrenders his rights, and in Jesus Christ — who gave up every right He had and made peace by His blood — He gives us an inheritance no eye has fully seen yet, a land and a world that cannot be lost, received the Abram way: by promise, not by grasping.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin draws a sharp contrast here between Lot's eyes and Abram's faith: Lot judged by present appearance and chose his own ruin, while Abram, content with God's promise, left the choice to God and lost nothing. Luther likewise praises Abraham's yielding as the fruit of faith — a man certain of God's word can afford to give way in earthly things, because his portion is secure where no herdsman can touch it.

**Reflection question:** Is there a choice in front of you right now that you are making the way Lot did — by how it looks — instead of asking what it will do to your walk with God?

## Genesis 14 — Melchizedek, King of Salem

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 14 gives us one of the clearest types of Christ in the Old Testament, and his name is Melchizedek. He appears out of nowhere — no genealogy, no beginning or end recorded — as both king of Salem and "priest of God Most High," a combination Israel's law would later forbid in one man. He brings out bread and wine, blesses Abram, and receives a tenth from him. David will sing of a coming priest "forever after the order of Melchizedek" in Psalm 110, and Hebrews 7 will take up this chapter at length to show that Jesus is that priest-king: greater than Abraham, since Abraham paid him the tithe; a priest not by descent but by God's oath; a king of righteousness and of peace — for that is what his name and his city mean.

We will keep the Hebrews 7 pointer brief here, as the New Testament itself will do the full work later. For Genesis, the point is simpler: centuries before Aaron, before Levi, before the law, God already had a priest-king in Jerusalem — Salem is Jerusalem — who blessed the father of the faithful with bread and wine. Christ's priesthood is not an afterthought or a backup plan. It is older than the law, greater than Abraham, and it was standing in Jerusalem, in type and shadow, all along.

### Commentary

**The war of the kings (14:1–12).** Chapter 14 opens like a dispatch from an ancient battlefield: four kings from the east against five kings of the Jordan Valley. The eastern kings have held the valley in tribute for twelve years; in the thirteenth, the valley kings rebel; in the fourteenth, the eastern kings come down to crush them, and do. The battle in the Valley of Siddim — full of bitumen pits — ends with Sodom and Gomorrah sacked, their goods carried off, and one prisoner who drags Abram into the story: "they also took Lot, the son of Abram's brother, who was dwelling in Sodom, and his possessions, and went their way." Dwell on that phrase: *dwelling in Sodom*. In chapter 13 Lot pitched his tents *near* Sodom. Now he lives in it. The drift has done its work, and the drift now costs him everything — home, goods, freedom. He is carried off as spoil by foreign kings, and the only reason his story does not end in a slave caravan is that his uncle still prays, still watches, and still rides.

**Abram the rescuer (14:13–16).** A fugitive escapes and brings the news to "Abram the Hebrew" — the first use of that name in Scripture — living by the oaks of Mamre. Abram's response is immediate and costly. He leads out his trained men, 318 of them, born in his own house, and pursues the victorious armies as far as Dan — over a hundred miles — divides his forces, attacks by night, and routs them, pursuing them beyond Damascus. "Then he brought back all the possessions, and also brought back his kinsman Lot with his possessions, and the women and the people." Abram is no passive dreamer here; the man of faith is also a man of action, and he risks his life and his household for the nephew who chose Sodom over him. That is grace in boots. Lot had taken the best land and left. Abram owed him nothing by the world's ledger. But kinsman love does not keep that ledger — and in this rescue we get a small preview of the greater Kinsman who would come, who pursues and recovers people who wandered away from Him, at far greater cost than a night march.

**Melchizedek, king and priest (14:17–20).** On the way home, two kings come out to meet Abram, and the contrast between them is the heart of the chapter. First — though Moses tells it second — there is the king of Sodom, coming with a bargain. But before him steps a stranger: "And Melchizedek king of Salem brought out bread and wine. (He was priest of God Most High.)" Out of a Canaanite city, in a land of idolatry, stands a king who serves the same God Abram serves, under the name God Most High, Possessor of heaven and earth. He refreshes the weary warrior with bread and wine, and he blesses him: "Blessed be Abram by God Most High, Possessor of heaven and earth; and blessed be God Most High, who has delivered your enemies into your hand!" Every word matters. The victory was God's, not Abram's 318. And Abram, the promise-bearer, the man God blessed in chapter 12, kneels under this priest's blessing and gives him "a tenth of everything." Abram recognized a priesthood greater than his own standing. For the weary and battle-worn, this scene is a meal in itself: after the fight, God sends refreshment and blessing through His priest — and Christ our Priest still meets His people after their battles with bread and wine and a blessing.

**Abram refuses Sodom's spoil (14:21–24).** Then the king of Sodom makes his offer: "Give me the persons, but take the goods for yourself." Keep the wealth, in other words — enrich yourself from Sodom. It sounds reasonable, even generous. But Abram has already lifted his hand to the LORD, God Most High, Possessor of heaven and earth, and sworn an oath: "I will not take a thread or a sandal strap or anything that is yours, lest you should say, 'I have made Abram rich.'" He will not owe Sodom anything. He will not let a wicked king claim credit for the blessing of God. His only exception is fair dealing: what his men have eaten, and the shares owed his allies Aner, Eshcol, and Mamre. There is a line Abram draws here that every believer in recovery should memorize: some money, some offers, some open doors cost more than they pay. Abram walked away from a fortune with empty hands and a clean oath, because he knew who his Provider was. The man who had just been blessed by Melchizedek did not need to be enriched by Sodom.

### Key verses

> "And Melchizedek king of Salem brought out bread and wine. (He was priest of God Most High.)" — Genesis 14:18 (ESV)

> "And he blessed him and said, 'Blessed be Abram by God Most High, Possessor of heaven and earth;'" — Genesis 14:19 (ESV)

> "and blessed be God Most High, who has delivered your enemies into your hand!' And Abram gave him a tenth of everything." — Genesis 14:20 (ESV)

> "But Abram said to the king of Sodom, 'I have lifted my hand to the LORD, God Most High, Possessor of heaven and earth, that I would not take a thread or a sandal strap or anything that is yours, lest you should say, "I have made Abram rich."'" — Genesis 14:22–23 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that drift is expensive — Lot chose Sodom step by step until Sodom owned his address, his goods, and finally his freedom, and sin's captivities are always more than we can fight our way out of alone — but the bad news points to the good news: God raises up a rescuer for the captive and a Priest for the weary; Abram rode through the night for a nephew who had wandered off, and Jesus, our great Priest-King after the order of Melchizedek, has pursued and recovered us at the cost of His own blood, and now meets us after every battle not with Sodom's bargains but with blessing, bread, and wine.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin treats Melchizedek with deliberate restraint, refusing the wild speculations some built on his silence, and holding to what Moses gives: a true king and priest of the true God, set before Abram — and before us — as a figure of Christ's unique office. Spurgeon, preaching on this priest-king, loved to linger on the refreshment of weary saints: Melchizedek appears precisely when Abram returns exhausted from battle, and so, Spurgeon urged, Christ ever meets His tired people with sustenance and blessing at the moment of their need.

**Reflection question:** What is your Sodom still offering you — money, approval, an old life with a price tag — and what would it mean to say, with Abram, "not a thread, not a sandal strap"?


## Genesis 15 — Counted as Righteousness

### Jesus in this chapter

Genesis 15:6 is the verse the whole New Testament leans on to explain how a sinner is made right with God: "And he believed the LORD, and he counted it to him as righteousness." Paul quotes it in Romans 4 and Galatians 3. James quotes it. The righteousness Abram received was not earned by his obedience, his circumcision — that has not even been given yet — or his record, which already includes the failure in Egypt. It was counted to him because he believed God's promise. And the promise he believed was, at its heart, a promise about an offspring. Paul says in Galatians 3 that the promises were made to Abraham and to his offspring, and that offspring is Christ. So when Abram believes, he is trusting in the coming Jesus — and God counts that trust as righteousness. That is the same way a sinner is saved today: not by cleaning up first, but by believing God's promise in Christ.

Jesus is also seen in the covenant ceremony that closes the chapter. When the smoking fire pot and flaming torch — God Himself, in fire as He will later appear in the burning bush and on Sinai — pass between the cut pieces *alone*, God is picturing the gospel. In an ancient covenant, both parties walked between the pieces, saying in effect, "May this happen to me if I break my word." Here only God walks. The keeping of this covenant rests on God alone — and at the cross, its curse fell on Jesus, so that its blessing could fall on us.

### Commentary

**"Fear not, Abram, I am your shield" (15:1–3).** The chapter opens "after these things" — after the battle, after Melchizedek, after Abram refused Sodom's riches and walked away from a fortune with empty hands. Now, in a vision, God speaks the words He so often speaks to His people: "Fear not, Abram, I am your shield; your reward shall be very great." Abram's answer is honest, and we should be grateful Moses recorded it. "O Lord GOD, what will you give me, for I continue childless, and the heir of my house is Eliezer of Damascus?" In plain words: *Lord, what good is reward without the son You promised? My heir is a servant.* This is not unbelief throwing the promise back in God's face. This is a believer bringing his ache straight to the God who made the promise. There is a kind of prayer that sounds almost like arguing, and God honors it here. If you have ever said, "Lord, I believe You — but look at my life," you are praying in Abram's company. Faith does not pretend the pain is not there. Faith takes the pain to the Promiser.

**Stars too many to count (15:4–6).** God's answer is direct: "This man shall not be your heir; your very own son shall be your heir." Then He does something tender and unforgettable. He brings Abram outside and says, "Look toward heaven, and number the stars, if you are able to number them... So shall your offspring be." God preaches to Abram through his eyes. A childless old man stands under a desert sky, looking at what he cannot count, and hears that his family will be like that. And then comes the verse that changed the world: "And he believed the LORD, and he counted it to him as righteousness." Abram had no child in his arms, no evidence in his circumstances, nothing but God's word and a sky full of stars — and he believed. Mark the order, because the order is the gospel: Abram believed, and *then* God counted. Righteousness was reckoned on the basis of faith, centuries before the law was given. He was not declared righteous because he was righteous in himself. He was declared righteous because he trusted God. That is still the only door in.

**"How am I to know?" and the cut covenant (15:7–11).** God restates the land promise — "I am the LORD who brought you out from Ur of the Chaldeans to give you this land to possess" — and Abram asks a second honest question: "O Lord GOD, how am I to know that I shall possess it?" God does not rebuke the question. He answers it with a covenant. He tells Abram to bring a heifer, a female goat, a ram, a turtledove, and a young pigeon. Abram cuts the animals in two and lays the halves opposite each other, making a path of blood between the pieces. Then he waits — and waiting, as anyone who has waited on God knows, invites attack. Birds of prey come down on the carcasses, and Abram drives them away. It is a small detail with a long reach: while you wait for God to confirm His promise, things will swoop in to snatch it — doubts, old voices, old appetites. Abram's job in the waiting was not to make the promise happen. It was to guard what God had given and drive off what came to steal it.

**The smoking fire pot covenant (15:12–21).** As the sun goes down, a deep sleep falls on Abram, and "a dreadful and great darkness" — God lets him feel, in his own body, the weight of the centuries ahead. Then God speaks the future plainly. Abram's offspring will be sojourners in a land not theirs — Egypt — enslaved and afflicted for 400 years. God does not hide the hard part of the promise. But judgment will fall on the nation that enslaves them, and they will come out "with great possessions." Abram himself will die in peace at a good old age. The land's current inhabitants are named, border to border, and their iniquity, God says, "is not yet complete" — even in judgment, God is patient, giving the Amorites generations more time. Then, when the sun has set and it is fully dark, the moment arrives: "behold, a smoking fire pot and a flaming torch passed between these pieces." God alone passes through. Abram does not walk that bloody path. The Hebrew word for this covenant is *berith* — a binding promise God cuts and God keeps. If the covenant depended on Abram's walking, it would die with Abram's failures, and he will fail again before Genesis is done. It depends on God, who walked it alone — and who, in Jesus, would one day bear its curse alone too. That is why this promise still stands for people whose own record would sink it.

### Key verses

> "After these things the word of the LORD came to Abram in a vision: 'Fear not, Abram, I am your shield; your reward shall be very great.'" — Genesis 15:1 (ESV)

> "And he brought him outside and said, 'Look toward heaven, and number the stars, if you are able to number them.' Then he said to him, 'So shall your offspring be.'" — Genesis 15:5 (ESV)

> "And he believed the LORD, and he counted it to him as righteousness." — Genesis 15:6 (ESV)

> "When the sun had gone down and it was dark, behold, a smoking fire pot and a flaming torch passed between these pieces." — Genesis 15:17 (ESV)

> "On that day the LORD made a covenant with Abram, saying, 'To your offspring I give this land, from the river of Egypt to the great river, the river Euphrates.'" — Genesis 15:18 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that we bring God the same empty hands Abram did — childless promises, a servant for an heir, a record with Egypt already in it — and if being right with God depended on our performance, our walking between the pieces, we would fall under the covenant's curse, because we break our word to God far more often than we keep it — but the bad news points to the good news: God walked the path alone, counted a believing sinner righteous on the spot, and in Jesus Christ He bore the curse Himself, so that righteousness is still counted today to anyone who simply believes the promise — not the strong, not the cleaned-up, but the trusting.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther, in his Lectures on Genesis, treats Genesis 15:6 as a chief article of the Christian faith, insisting that Abraham was justified by faith alone, apart from works, and that the same reckoning is offered to every believer in Christ. Calvin likewise stresses that Abraham's righteousness was imputed — placed to his account by God's gracious counting, not found in Abraham himself — and that the one-sided covenant ceremony proves the promise rests on God's faithfulness from first to last.

**Reflection question:** When God says "Fear not, I am your shield," do you answer Him honestly with your real ache the way Abram did — or have you stopped bringing that ache to Him at all?

## Genesis 16 — Hagar and the God Who Sees

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is seen in this chapter first in the mercy that runs ahead of the promise. Hagar is not in the covenant line. She is an Egyptian slave girl, used by Abram's household, abused by Sarai's jealousy, and driven out into the wilderness pregnant and alone. And it is to *her* — before almost anyone else in Scripture — that the angel of the LORD appears, speaks her name, gives her a promise about her son, and hears her affliction. The God of Abram is not a tribal God who only notices the important people. He sees the runaway slave in the desert. That is the heart Jesus will display when He stops for the Samaritan woman at a well, notices a widow putting in two small coins, and touches people nobody else would touch. The gospel was always going to reach outsiders, because God was always this kind of God.

There is also a careful point to make about the angel of the LORD. Hagar, after the encounter, names not just the place but God Himself: "You are a God of seeing," and Moses adds that she spoke of the LORD "who spoke to her." Many Reformed readers, Calvin included, have understood appearances of the angel of the LORD — who speaks as God and is addressed as God — as appearances of the Son before His incarnation. We hold that with appropriate care: the text's own emphasis is Hagar's, that the LORD Himself found her. Either way, the comfort stands — the God who would one day come in the flesh to seek the lost was already seeking them in the wilderness of Genesis 16.

### Commentary

**A shortcut that seemed reasonable (16:1–3).** The chapter opens with the fact that has hung over the story since chapter 11: "Now Sarai, Abram's wife, had borne him no children." Ten years have passed since the promise. Ten years of waiting will wear down almost anyone. Sarai has an Egyptian maidservant, Hagar, and Sarai proposes the accepted custom of the day: Abram will take Hagar, and Sarai will "obtain children by her" — build a family through the slave's body. Notice the echo of Eden in her words and his response: Sarai speaks, and Abram "listened to the voice of Sarai," the same pattern as Adam listening to Eve. The plan made cultural sense. Everybody did it. It was practical, it was available, and it bypassed the one thing God was asking for: trust in His timing. That is what makes this chapter so painfully familiar. Most of our worst detours were not rebellions we planned in the dark. They were shortcuts that looked reasonable in the light — ways to get God's promise on our schedule, with our methods, sparing us the waiting. Sarai and Abram were trying to help God keep His promise. The wreckage that follows is what "helping God" usually produces.

**Favor turns to friction (16:4–6).** The plan works — and immediately poisons everything. Hagar conceives, and she "looked with contempt" on her mistress. The slave girl, used as an instrument, now holds the one thing Sarai wants most, and pride grows in the crack that sin opened. Sarai, in turn, blames Abram: "May the wrong done to me be on you!" — though the scheme was hers — and Abram, passive for the second time in the story, washes his hands: "Behold, your servant is in your power; do to her as you please." Three people, one shortcut, and everyone is now either proud, bitter, or absent. Sarai treats Hagar so harshly that the pregnant girl runs away into the wilderness. Sin never stays in the room where it started. A compromise made in the marriage tent ends with a runaway in the desert, an unborn child at risk, and a household at war with itself.
