Moses does not soften any of it, because he wants us to feel the full weight of what one "reasonable" unbelief can set in motion.

**The angel finds Hagar (16:7–12).** The next sentence is one of the most beautiful in Genesis: "The angel of the LORD found her by a spring of water in the wilderness." He *found* her. She was not looking for God; God came looking for her — as He looked for Adam in the garden, as the shepherd looks for the sheep. He calls her by name — "Hagar, servant of Sarai" — the first time her name is spoken with honor in the story, and He asks two questions every runaway needs to hear: "Where have you come from and where are you going?" She can only answer the first: "I am fleeing from my mistress Sarai." She has no idea where she is going. Then comes a hard word wrapped in a great promise. He sends her back — back to submit to Sarai, not because Sarai was right, but because the wilderness was death for her and her child, and God's provision for her was, for now, in that household. But she does not go back empty. "I will surely multiply your offspring so that they cannot be numbered for multitude." And her son will be named Ishmael — "God hears" — "because the LORD has listened to your affliction." The description of Ishmael's future is honest, not flattering: a wild donkey of a man, his hand against everyone. God does not lie to Hagar to comfort her. He gives her truth, a name, a promise, and His presence — which turns out to be enough.

**"You are a God of seeing" (16:13–16).** Hagar's response gives this chapter its glory. "So she called the name of the LORD who spoke to her, 'You are a God of seeing,' for she said, 'Truly here I have seen him who looks after me.'" An Egyptian slave girl becomes the first person in Scripture to give God a name — and the name she gives Him comes straight out of her own rescue: the God who *sees*. Not the God who only sees the patriarchs. The God who sees *me*. The well is named Beer-lahai-roi, the well of the Living One who sees me. She goes back, bears Abram a son, and Abram names him Ishmael — obeying, at last, a word from God that came to the household through the slave girl. Abram is eighty-six years old. The promise of God has survived the family's worst idea, a runaway has come home with a testimony, and a boy named "God hears" is born in Abram's house. But Ishmael is not the child of promise, as the next chapter will make plain. God's mercy to Ishmael is real. The covenant line is another matter — and God keeps both truths without confusing them.

### Key verses

> "The angel of the LORD found her by a spring of water in the wilderness, the spring on the way to Shur." — Genesis 16:7 (ESV)

> "And the angel of the LORD said to her, 'Behold, you are pregnant and shall bear a son. You shall call his name Ishmael, because the LORD has listened to your affliction.'" — Genesis 16:11 (ESV)

> "So she called the name of the LORD who spoke to her, 'You are a God of seeing,' for she said, 'Truly here I have seen him who looks after me.'" — Genesis 16:13 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that our shortcuts around God's timing always bill us later — Sarai's reasonable plan produced contempt, blame, cruelty, and a pregnant girl running into a desert to die, and every attempt to force God's promise on our schedule leaves people wounded in the same ways — but the bad news points to the good news: the God we wound each other running from is a God who finds runaways by name, who hears affliction and sees the person everybody else used, and that same seeing, hearing God came in Jesus Christ to find people in their wilderness and bring them home alive.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, on this chapter, is unsparing about Abraham and Sarah's unbelief in taking Hagar, yet lingers on God's gentleness toward Hagar herself — a foreign slave, comforted and promised blessing — as proof that God's grace reaches beyond the visible church's household. Luther likewise draws out the comfort of Ishmael's name: God hears affliction, even the affliction of those far outside the promise, and no sufferer's cry falls outside His attention.

**Reflection question:** When you have run — from a hard situation, from people, from God Himself — what would it mean for you to stop and answer His two questions honestly: where have you come from, and where are you going?

## Genesis 17 — The Covenant of Circumcision

### Jesus in this chapter

Thirteen years of silence pass between Genesis 16 and Genesis 17, and when God speaks again, He speaks the promise into its final, sharpened form — and every sharpening points to Jesus. The son will come not through Ishmael but through Sarah, and from him will come nations and kings. Paul explains in Romans 4 that Abraham is therefore the father not of one bloodline only, but "of all who believe" — the father of a multitude gathered by faith in Christ. When God renames Abram "Abraham, father of a multitude of nations," He is describing, centuries early, the worldwide family that Jesus will gather from every tribe and tongue. You may be part of that multitude right now, reading about your own family name being given.

The sign of the covenant also points forward. Circumcision was an outward sign of an inward reality — a heart set apart for God, which Moses and the prophets would later demand outright: circumcise your hearts. Paul in Colossians 2 says believers have received the true circumcision "made without hands" in Christ. The sign changed; the reality it pictured — God marking a people as His own, by His own work — comes to its fullness in Jesus.

### Commentary

**"I am God Almighty" (17:1–2).** "When Abram was ninety-nine years old the LORD appeared to Abram and said to him, 'I am God Almighty; walk before me, and be blameless, that I may make my covenant between me and you, and may multiply you greatly.'" Thirteen years have passed since Ishmael's birth — thirteen years in which, as far as the record shows, God said nothing. If you are in a long silence right now, this chapter was written for you. When God finally speaks, He introduces Himself by a name Abram needs for an impossible promise: God Almighty — El Shaddai, the God for whom a ninety-nine-year-old man and an eighty-nine-year-old woman having a baby is not a problem. Then comes the call: "walk before me, and be blameless." Not sinless perfection as a price for the promise — Abram was already counted righteous by faith in chapter 15 — but a life lived openly before God's face, holding nothing back. Abram falls on his face. That is the right posture for what is coming.

**Names changed: Abram becomes Abraham (17:3–8).** God now expands the covenant in a rush of "I wills." Kings will come from him. The covenant will be everlasting, "to be God to you and to your offspring after you" — that phrase, *to be God to you*, is the beating heart of every covenant God makes. The land is given "for an everlasting possession." And Abram gets a new name: "father of a multitude of nations." God renames a childless ninety-nine-year-old man "father of multitudes" and expects him to answer to it — to introduce himself by a promise he cannot yet see. That is how God works. He calls things that are not as though they were. He gives broken people a new name — forgiven, beloved, clean — before they feel like it is true. Identity in the covenant is received, not achieved.

**The sign of circumcision (17:9–14).** Then God gives the covenant a sign to be carried in the body: every male in Abraham's household — born in the house or bought with money, family and servant and foreigner alike — is to be circumcised. "So shall my covenant be in your flesh an everlasting covenant." The sign is deliberately not hidden in a book or worn on a sleeve; it is in the flesh, at the very place of generation, because the promise concerns offspring — God marking the source of life itself as belonging to Him. It is also a sign applied to infants who could not choose or earn it. Before a boy in Abraham's house could do anything for God, God set His mark on him by grace. The severity is real too: the uncircumcised male "shall be cut off from his people; he has broken my covenant." To refuse the sign was to refuse the covenant itself. Signs matter to God — they are His appointed way of sealing His promise to His people.

**Sarai becomes Sarah — and Abraham laughs (17:15–22).** Now, for the first time, God names Sarah herself in the promise: "As for Sarai your wife, you shall not call her name Sarai, but Sarah shall be her name. I will bless her, and moreover, I will give you a son by her... kings of peoples shall come from her." No more shortcuts. No more Hagar plans. The son comes through the barren wife, or he does not come at all. Abraham's response is gloriously human: he falls on his face *and laughs* — "Shall a child be born to a man who is a hundred years old? Shall Sarah, who is ninety years old, bear a child?" It is not mocking laughter; it is the laughter of a man doing the math and finding the promise absurd and wonderful at once. And alongside it is a father's heart: "Oh that Ishmael might live before you!" God hears that prayer — Ishmael will be blessed and made a great nation — but the covenant line is set: the son is Isaac, which means *he laughs*. God takes Abraham's stunned laughter and builds it into the boy's name forever.

**Same-day obedience (17:23–27).** The chapter ends not with more talking but with doing: "Then Abraham took Ishmael his son and all those born in his house or bought with his money, every male among the men of Abraham's house, and he circumcised the flesh of their foreskins that very day, as God had said to him." *That very day.* Abraham is ninety-nine. Ishmael is thirteen. Every male in a large household undergoes a painful, costly obedience — immediately, completely, including Abraham himself. The man who once washed his hands of Hagar now leads his whole house into the covenant sign without delay. Faith counted righteous shows itself in feet that move the same day God speaks. Genesis 17 closes with a renamed, marked, laughing old man who has nothing to show for the promise yet except a name, a scar, and God's word. It was enough. It still is.

### Key verses

> "When Abram was ninety-nine years old the LORD appeared to Abram and said to him, 'I am God Almighty; walk before me, and be blameless, that I may make my covenant between me and you, and may multiply you greatly.'" — Genesis 17:1 (ESV)

> "No longer shall your name be called Abram, but your name shall be Abraham, for I have made you the father of a multitude of nations." — Genesis 17:5 (ESV)

> "And I will establish my covenant between me and you and your offspring after you throughout their generations for an everlasting covenant, to be God to you and to your offspring after you." — Genesis 17:7 (ESV)

> "And God said to Abraham, 'As for Sarai your wife, you shall not call her name Sarai, but Sarah shall be her name.'" — Genesis 17:15 (ESV)

> "God said, 'No, but Sarah your wife shall bear you a son, and you shall call his name Isaac. I will establish my covenant with him as an everlasting covenant for his offspring after him.'" — Genesis 17:19 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that thirteen silent years and a body as good as dead are our true condition before God — we cannot produce the life He promises, our best schemes give us Ishmaels instead of Isaacs, and left to ourselves the promise would die of old age in us — but the bad news points to the good news: God Almighty specializes in life from dead places; He renames the childless "father of a multitude," writes His covenant on people before they can earn it, and in Jesus Christ — born, in God's timing, to fulfill this very covenant — He gives every believer a new name and an everlasting God who says, and keeps saying, "I will be God to you."

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, commenting on this chapter, emphasizes that circumcision was given to Abraham *after* he was justified by faith, proving the sign seals a righteousness already received by believing — it does not create it. Luther, in his Lectures on Genesis, dwells on Abraham's laughter as the honest response of faith wrestling with an impossible promise, and on the new name as pure gospel: God declares Abraham to be what he is not yet in himself, and His declaration makes it so.

**Reflection question:** What new name has God given you in Christ — forgiven, beloved, His own — that you are still not answering to, because you are waiting to feel it first?

## Genesis 18 — The Three Visitors and the Intercession

### Jesus in this chapter

Three men come to Abraham's tent in the heat of the day, and Abraham runs to meet them, bows low, and sets a feast before them. The text itself calls the visitor "the LORD" — "And the LORD appeared to him by the oaks of Mamre" (18:1). Many Reformed readers through the centuries have seen, with care, the LORD Himself among the three, with the other two as angels who go on toward Sodom in chapter 19. We should not be more dogmatic than Scripture is, but we also should not miss what is plain: God came near. He ate at a sinner's table. He spoke face to face with His friend. Long before Bethlehem, the Lord was already drawing near to dwell with His people, and every such visit points forward to the day when the Word would become flesh and dwell among us in truth.

Christ is also here in the intercession. Abraham stands before the LORD and pleads for a wicked city — "Will you indeed sweep away the righteous with the wicked?" That picture of a man standing between God and sinners, asking for mercy, is a shadow of the greater Intercessor. Abraham pleads and finally stops. Jesus pleads and never stops. "He always lives to make intercession" for His people (Hebrews 7:25). And Christ is here in the promise itself, spoken again over Sarah's laughter: a son is coming. Isaac is not the Savior, but every step toward Isaac is a step toward the promised Seed through whom all the families of the earth would be blessed.

### Commentary

**The LORD at Abraham's tent (18:1–8).** Abraham is sitting at the door of his tent in the heat of the day when he looks up and sees three men standing near him. He does not wait. He runs, bows himself to the earth, and begs them to stay — a little water, a washing of feet, a morsel of bread, rest under the tree. Then he hurries: Sarah is to make cakes from fine flour, a servant is to prepare a tender calf, and Abraham sets curds and milk and meat before his guests and stands by them while they eat. This is more than Middle Eastern courtesy, though it is that. Abraham receives these visitors the way a man receives God — with haste, with honor, and with the best he has. There is a lesson here for worn-out people: the Lord often comes in the ordinary heat of an ordinary day, at the tent door, not in the storm. Abraham was not looking for a visitation. He was sitting down. And God was standing near him.

**The promise spoken again — and Sarah laughs (18:9–15).** The visitors ask for Sarah, and the promise is spoken once more, now with a time attached: "I will surely return to you about this time next year, and Sarah your wife shall have a son." Sarah is listening at the tent door behind him — ninety years old, long past the age of childbearing — and she laughs to herself: "After I am worn out, and my lord is old, shall I have pleasure?" Notice the honesty of this Bible. It does not clean Sarah up. Her laughter is the laughter of a woman who has buried this hope a hundred times, who has carried disappointment so long that hope itself sounds like a joke. Many who read this know that laugh. It is not rebellion exactly. It is a heart that has learned not to hope, because hoping hurts. Then comes the word that carries the whole chapter — and the whole Bible: "Is anything too hard for the LORD?" The Hebrew word behind "too hard" carries the sense of too wonderful, too marvelous for God to do. Nothing is too wonderful for Him. When Sarah denies laughing — "I did not laugh," for she was afraid — the LORD answers with a gentleness that also tells the truth: "No, but you did laugh." God names our unbelief honestly, and He keeps His promise anyway. That is grace. The promise does not rest on Sarah's faith being strong. It rests on God's word being true.

**The LORD takes Abraham into His counsel (18:16–21).** As the men rise and look toward Sodom, the LORD speaks aloud, as it were, in Abraham's hearing: "Shall I hide from Abraham what I am about to do?" God draws His friend into His purposes, and He gives His reason — Abraham will become a great nation, all the nations will be blessed in him, and Abraham is to command his household to keep the way of the LORD by doing righteousness and justice. Election is never for privilege alone; it is for a way of life passed down at the kitchen table. Then the LORD speaks of Sodom: the outcry against the city is great and its sin is very grave. God is not acting on rumor. "I will go down to see" — the Judge of all the earth investigates before He strikes. Judgment in Scripture is never hasty, never careless, never a temper. It comes only after long patience and full knowledge.

**The intercession (18:22–33).** The two men turn toward Sodom, but Abraham still stands before the LORD — and then Abraham draws near and prays one of the boldest prayers in the Bible. Will God sweep away the righteous with the wicked? Suppose there are fifty righteous in the city — will He not spare the place for their sake? "Far be it from you to do such a thing, to put the righteous to death with the wicked... Shall not the Judge of all the earth do what is just?" Abraham is not bargaining with a merchant; he is clinging to the character of God. He appeals to who God is. And the LORD answers: for fifty, He will spare the whole place. Then Abraham presses down — forty-five, forty, thirty, twenty, ten — each time with deeper humility: "I who am but dust and ashes." Each time the LORD grants the plea. Abraham is dust, and he knows it, and still he prays like a friend of God, because that is what God has made him. The prayer ends at ten, and the LORD goes His way, and Abraham returns to his place. We learn later that there were not even ten. But we also learn something greater: God delights to be pleaded with. He invites the intercession He intends to answer. If you are praying for someone who seems too far gone — a child, a spouse, a friend still in the chains you once wore — this chapter tells you to keep standing before the LORD. Dust and ashes can plead, and the Judge of all the earth will do what is just, and He is more merciful than we dare to ask.

### Key verses

> "Is anything too hard for the LORD? At the appointed time I will return to you, about this time next year, and Sarah shall have a son." — Genesis 18:14 (ESV)

> "Shall I hide from Abraham what I am about to do, seeing that Abraham shall surely become a great and mighty nation, and all the nations of the earth shall be blessed in him?" — Genesis 18:17–18 (ESV)

> "Far be it from you to do such a thing, to put the righteous to death with the wicked, so that the righteous fare as the wicked! Far be that from you! Shall not the Judge of all the earth do what is just?" — Genesis 18:25 (ESV)

> "Behold, I have undertaken to speak to the Lord, I who am but dust and ashes." — Genesis 18:27 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that Sodom is real, its sin is very grave, and the Judge of all the earth will not call evil good forever — and if we are honest, we know there is a Sodom in every human heart that has laughed at God's promise and settled for lesser pleasures. But the good news is standing right beside it in this chapter: the same LORD who judges is the LORD who comes to a sinner's tent, eats at his table, repeats His promise over a doubting woman's laughter, and invites dust and ashes to plead for the lost — and in the fullness of time He sent His own Son, the greater Intercessor, who does not plead for fifty righteous or ten, but who Himself bore the judgment so that wicked cities and worn-out sinners could be spared for His sake.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, commenting on this chapter, lingered over the LORD's question — "Shall I hide from Abraham what I am about to do?" — and saw in it God's fatherly kindness in taking His servant into His confidence, teaching us that prayer is not informing God of what He does not know but being drawn into what He has purposed to do. Luther likewise treasured Abraham's boldness in prayer as a model for believers: we come as dust and ashes, yet we come boldly, because God Himself has invited us near.

**Reflection question:** Who is the one person you have nearly stopped praying for — and what would it look like to stand before the LORD for them again this week, trusting that nothing is too hard for Him?

## Genesis 19 — Sodom Destroyed, Lot Rescued

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus Himself preached from this chapter. "Remember Lot's wife," He said (Luke 17:32) — three words that turn this whole story into a warning for anyone who looks back to the life God is calling them out of. And when Jesus described His own return, He reached for Sodom: people eating, drinking, buying, planting, building — ordinary life, unprepared hearts — and then the day came (Luke 17:28–30). So Christ stands over this chapter as its Interpreter. He does not treat Sodom as an embarrassing old story. He treats it as a mirror and an alarm clock for every generation, including ours.

But mercy is here too, and mercy points to Christ. Lot is rescued not because he is righteous in himself — this chapter shows how compromised he has become — but because "God remembered Abraham" (19:29). Lot is saved for another man's sake, through another man's prayers. That is a picture of the gospel itself. No believer is saved because God remembered our goodness. We are saved because God remembered His covenant, and because Another — greater than Abraham — interceded, and because God delivered us for His sake. Peter says the Lord knows how to rescue the godly from trials while holding the unrighteous for judgment (2 Peter 2:9), and he holds up Lot as the proof of the rescue half of that sentence.

### Commentary

**The angels at Sodom's gate (19:1–11).** The two angels arrive at Sodom in the evening, and Lot is sitting in the gate — the place of business and influence. He has moved from pitching his tent near Sodom (13:12) to living in Sodom (14:12) to sitting in its gate. Compromise rarely happens in one leap; it happens by degrees, and Lot's story is the Bible's slow-motion picture of it. Still, Lot has learned Abraham's hospitality: he bows, urges the visitors to come under his roof, washes their feet, and makes a feast. But the city shows its heart that night. The men of Sodom, both young and old, surround the house and demand that the guests be brought out "that we may know them." Lot goes out and offers the unthinkable — his own daughters — a desperate, shameful bargain that shows how deeply Sodom's thinking has rotted even the man who is called righteous. The mob presses in, accusing Lot of being a sojourner who would play the judge, and the angels pull him inside and strike the men at the door with blindness. Even blinded, the men "wearied themselves groping for the door" — sin does not quit when it cannot see; it gropes on in the dark. Anyone who has known addiction knows that blindness. The craving does not reason. It gropes.

**The warning and the lingering (19:12–22).** The angels tell Lot plainly: get your family out, for we will destroy this place; the outcry has become great before the LORD. Lot goes to his sons-in-law, and here is one of the saddest lines in Genesis: "he seemed to his sons-in-law to be jesting." A compromised witness has no weight when the warning finally comes. Years of blending in had cost Lot his voice. Then morning comes — and Lot lingers. The judgment is announced, the angels are standing there, and Lot lingers. So the angels seize him by the hand — him, his wife, his two daughters — "the LORD being merciful to him," and drag his family out of the city. Hear that clearly: Lot's rescue is not Lot's decisiveness. It is God's mercy taking a lingering man by the hand. Some of us are saved exactly that way. We did not run out of our Sodom with great resolve; the Lord seized our hand and pulled us out while we were still looking around. The command is urgent: "Escape for your life. Do not look back or stop anywhere in the valley. Escape to the hills, lest you be swept away." Lot bargains even here — let me flee to little Zoar instead — and God grants even that. Mercy upon mercy, patience upon patience, toward a man who gives God very little to work with.

**Fire from heaven — and a wife who looked back (19:23–29).** As the sun rises, the LORD rains sulfur and fire on Sodom and Gomorrah, and the smoke of the land goes up like the smoke of a furnace. This is not myth to soften or explain away; Scripture presents it as real judgment on real, grave, unrepented sin — violence, pride, arrogance, and contempt for the needy, as Ezekiel later sums it up (Ezekiel 16:49). And on the road out, Lot's wife looks back — and becomes a pillar of salt. One look. Her body left Sodom, but her heart had stayed, and the backward look told the truth. Jesus' warning stands for every person being delivered from an old life: do not look back with longing at what God is burning behind you. The old crowd, the old places, the old habits will call your name. Looking back is not a small thing when your life depends on going forward. Then the chapter gives us Abraham at dawn, standing where he had stood before the LORD, looking at the smoke — and the summary line that explains Lot's survival: "So it was that, when God destroyed the cities of the valley, God remembered Abraham and sent Lot out of the midst of the overthrow." Abraham's intercession did something. It did not save the city, but it saved the man. Your prayers for the person still inside are not wasted breath.

**Lot in the cave (19:30–38).** The chapter ends in darkness that we should not rush past. Lot, afraid even in Zoar, hides in a cave in the hills, and his daughters — shaped by Sodom's morals even after escaping Sodom's fire — get their father drunk on two successive nights and lie with him, and from those nights come Moab and Ben-ammi, fathers of the Moabites and Ammonites, peoples who would trouble Israel for centuries. Getting out of Sodom geographically is not the same as getting Sodom out of the heart. Rescue is the beginning, not the end; a man pulled from the fire still needs to be renewed, or the old world rebuilds itself in the cave. And yet — grace will have the last word even here, for Ruth the Moabitess, descended from this very line, will one day be folded into the family of the Messiah. God can bring a Redeemer's ancestor out of a cave of shame. No one's family tree is beyond His redemption.

### Key verses

> "But he lingered. So the men seized him and his wife and his two daughters by the hand, the LORD being merciful to him, and they brought him out and set him outside the city." — Genesis 19:16 (ESV)

> "Escape for your life. Do not look back or stop anywhere in the valley. Escape to the hills, lest you be swept away." — Genesis 19:17 (ESV)

> "So it was that, when God destroyed the cities of the valley, God remembered Abraham and sent Lot out of the midst of the overthrow, when he overthrew the cities in which Lot had lived." — Genesis 19:29 (ESV)

> "Then the LORD rained on Sodom and Gomorrah sulfur and fire from the LORD out of heaven." — Genesis 19:24 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that judgment is real, compromise is costly, and a person can be dragged out of a burning city with his heart still inside it — lingering, looking back, carrying the old world's ways into the cave. But the good news shines all the brighter against the smoke: the LORD is merciful to lingering people, He seizes the hand of the man who cannot save himself, He remembers His covenant and His interceding friend, and in Christ He has done far more than pull us out of the city — He has given us a new heart and a King who says, "Remember Lot's wife," not to crush us, but to keep us walking forward into life.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin treated Lot's lingering as a mirror of the sluggishness that clings to every believer — we are all, he observed, dragged to salvation more than we run to it, and the angels' grip on Lot's hand is a picture of God's persevering mercy toward His own. Spurgeon, preaching on "Remember Lot's wife," pressed the warning home to comfortable hearers: a person may go a long way with God's people, may even flee the city, and yet perish with a heart turned back toward sin.

**Reflection question:** Where are you still lingering or looking back — and what would it mean today to let the Lord take your hand and walk forward without turning around?


## Genesis 20 — Abraham and Abimelech

### Jesus in this chapter

Christ appears in this chapter not in a vision or a type, but in the way God guards the line of promise. Sarah is the woman through whom the promised Seed will come — the line that leads, generation by generation, to Jesus. When Sarah is taken into Abimelech's house, the whole future of redemption is, humanly speaking, in danger. And God acts. He comes to a pagan king in a dream, shuts up every womb in his household, and puts Sarah back where she belongs. The birth of Christ at the end of this long story was never hanging by a thread, because God never let go of the thread. Every quiet rescue like this one is part of how we got Bethlehem.

Jesus is also foreshadowed in Abraham's office here. God says to Abimelech of this fearful, stumbling Abraham: "he is a prophet, and he will pray for you, and you shall live" (20:7). The sinner who just failed becomes the intercessor through whom a king and his household are healed — "Abraham prayed to God, and God healed Abimelech" (20:17). That pattern reaches its fullness in Christ, the true Prophet, who intercedes for those who wronged Him and whose prayer actually opens what sin had closed. In Abraham the picture is smudged by the man's own sin. In Jesus it is spotless.

### Commentary

**"She is my sister" — again (20:1–2).** Abraham journeys toward the Negeb and settles between Kadesh and Shur, sojourning in Gerar. And there, the father of the faithful does the very thing he did in Egypt twenty years earlier: he says of Sarah, "She is my sister," and Abimelech king of Gerar sends and takes Sarah. Before we shake our heads too quickly, we should notice how Scripture is teaching us about the way old sins work. This was not a new temptation; it was an old groove. Abraham had a settled fear — "there is no fear of God at all in this place, and they will kill me because of my wife" (20:11) — and under pressure he slid back into the lie that had "worked" before. Many believers know this exact pattern. The old coping lie, the old escape, the old compromise does not die just because years have passed or because God has blessed us since. Abraham had just interceded for Sodom like a prince with God. He had heard the promise renewed with a date on it. And still fear ran his mouth. Growth is real, but so is the remaining weakness, and this chapter is God's kindness to every believer who has fallen into a sin they thought they were finished with: He does not hide His friend's failure, and He does not abandon His friend because of it.

**God comes to Abimelech in a dream (20:3–7).** God speaks to Abimelech at night with words that leave no room for misunderstanding: "Behold, you are a dead man because of the woman whom you have taken, for she is a man's wife." Abimelech pleads his integrity — he has not touched her; both Abraham and Sarah called her his sister; "in the integrity of my heart and the innocence of my hands I have done this." And God, remarkably, agrees with him: "Yes, I know that you have done this in the integrity of your heart, and it was I who kept you from sinning against me. Therefore I did not let you touch her." There is a whole doctrine in that sentence. God restrains sin. He kept this king from a sin the king did not even know he was near. How many times has the Lord done that for us — kept us from the touch, the click, the call, the relapse — in ways we will only learn in heaven? Then God commands restoration: give the man his wife back, "for he is a prophet, and he will pray for you, and you shall live." Judgment and mercy are woven together: the warning is severe, and the way of life is held open in the same breath.

**The confrontation and Abraham's confession (20:8–13).** Abimelech rises early, tells his servants, and summons Abraham with a question every backslider needs to hear from somebody: "What have you done to us? And how have I sinned against you, that you have brought on me and my kingdom a great sin? You have done to me things that ought not to be done." It is a hard day when a man outside the covenant has to rebuke a man inside it for his dishonesty. Abraham's answer is honest in its own way, and it exposes the root: he was afraid. He assumed there was no fear of God in Gerar — an assumption this chapter proves wrong, since Abimelech shows more fear of God than Abraham does. And Abraham explains the half-truth: Sarah really is his half-sister, the daughter of his father though not of his mother. A half-truth arranged to deceive is a lie wearing a disguise, and Abraham had been carrying this arrangement since Ur — "this is the kindness you must do me: at every place to which we come, say of me, 'He is my brother.'" Long-standing arrangements with dishonesty do not become righteous by becoming habits. God brings them into the light, sometimes through the mouth of the very people we wronged.

**Restoration, gifts, and a prophet's prayer (20:14–18).** Abimelech does far more than apologize. He gives Abraham sheep and oxen, male and female servants, freedom to dwell anywhere in his land, and a thousand pieces of silver given publicly for Sarah — "a sign of your innocence in the eyes of all." The wronged king acts with a generosity that shames the patriarch's fear. Sarah is vindicated before everyone's eyes. Then comes the verse the whole chapter bends toward: "Then Abraham prayed to God, and God healed Abimelech, and also healed his wife and female slaves so that they bore children." The man who caused the trouble becomes, by God's appointment, the man whose prayer ends it. God does not sideline His failing servant; He puts the prophet back on his feet and works through his prayer. "For the LORD had closed all the wombs of the house of Abimelech because of Sarah, Abraham's wife" — and now, through prayer, He opens them. For anyone who believes their failure has permanently disqualified them from being useful to God or to other people: read this ending twice. The intercessor of chapter 18 fell in chapter 20, and God still healed a household through his praying. Confession and restoration, not perfection, is the path back to usefulness.

### Key verses

> "Then God said to him in the dream, 'Yes, I know that you have done this in the integrity of your heart, and it was I who kept you from sinning against me. Therefore I did not let you touch her.'" — Genesis 20:6 (ESV)

> "Now then, return the man's wife, for he is a prophet, and he will pray for you, and you shall live. But if you do not return her, know that you shall surely die, you and all who are yours." — Genesis 20:7 (ESV)

> "Then Abraham prayed to God, and God healed Abimelech, and also healed his wife and female slaves so that they bore children." — Genesis 20:17 (ESV)

> "For the LORD had closed all the wombs of the house of Abimelech because of Sarah, Abraham's wife." — Genesis 20:18 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that even a friend of God can slide back into an old lie, that fear can make us assume the worst of others and endanger the very people we should protect, and that our quiet compromises bring trouble on households that never asked for it. But the good news is that God guards His promise better than we guard our own obedience: He restrains sin we cannot see, He speaks before disaster lands, He vindicates the wronged, and He takes the very man who fell and heals others through his prayer — and in Jesus, the spotless Prophet, that intercession never fails and never has to be restored, because He never falls.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin observed how gently God deals with Abimelech's ignorance while dealing so seriously with Abraham's dissimulation, noting that God measures sins not only by the outward act but by the light a person has received — which makes grace toward Abraham all the more striking, since the man with the most light stumbled and was still restored. The Reformers returned often to this chapter's comfort: God's covenant does not stand because His servants stand firm, but because He upholds both the promise and the promise-bearer.

**Reflection question:** What old groove — an old lie, an old escape, an old fear — do you slide into under pressure, and what would it mean to bring that arrangement into the light before God today instead of carrying it to the next place?

## Genesis 21 — Isaac Born, Ishmael Sent Out

### Jesus in this chapter

Isaac's birth is one of the great signposts to Jesus in all of Genesis. A child promised for decades is born to a ninety-year-old mother and a hundred-year-old father — a birth humanly impossible, accomplished entirely by the word and power of God. Paul seizes on this in Romans 4: Abraham's body was "as good as dead," yet God "gives life to the dead and calls into existence the things that do not exist." Isaac is not the virgin-born Son, but his birth trains God's people to expect a greater one: when God finally sent the true Seed, He again began with a woman who could not conceive — this time a virgin — because salvation has always been God's work from first to last, never man's achievement.

Christ is also glimpsed in the wilderness of Beersheba. Hagar and Ishmael are cast out, the water is gone, the boy is dying under a bush, and his mother cannot bear to watch. Then "God heard the voice of the boy" (21:17) — and the angel of God calls to Hagar from heaven, opens her eyes to a well of water, and repeats the promise over Ishmael. The God who hears the cry of the outcast boy is the same God who, in Jesus, would seek the lost, touch the untouchable, and say that whoever comes to Him He will never cast out. The gospel's welcome to outsiders did not begin in the Gospels. It was flowing already in this desert.

### Commentary

**The LORD visited Sarah (21:1–7).** The chapter opens with one of the most satisfying sentences in Scripture: "The LORD visited Sarah as he had said, and the LORD did to Sarah as he had promised." Everything hangs on those phrases — as He had said, as He had promised. Decades of waiting, a botched shortcut through Hagar, laughter of unbelief — and at the appointed time, God simply does what He said. Abraham is a hundred years old when Isaac is born. On the eighth day Abraham circumcises him, as God commanded, and names him Isaac — Laughter — because God has turned Sarah's bitter, hiding laughter into open joy: "God has made laughter for me; everyone who hears will laugh with me." Notice the change. In chapter 18 Sarah laughed in secret and denied it in fear. Now she laughs out loud and invites the world to laugh with her: "Who would have said to Abraham that Sarah would nurse children? Yet I have borne him a son in his old age." God does not merely fulfill promises; He redeems the very memories that once shamed us. The laugh that was once unbelief becomes testimony. Whatever chapter of your life you once hid in fear, God is able to bring it into the open as joy.

**The feast, the mocking, and the hard word (21:8–13).** The child grows and is weaned, and Abraham makes a great feast. But at the feast Sarah sees Ishmael — the son of Hagar the Egyptian, now a teenager — laughing, and the text suggests it was not innocent laughter; Paul reads it as persecution (Galatians 4:29). Sarah demands: "Cast out this slave woman with her son, for the son of this slave woman shall not be heir with my son Isaac." The word displeases Abraham greatly — this is his son too, his firstborn, the child of his own scheme, and he loves him. Family pain in this chapter is real and should not be flattened. But God speaks to Abraham: "Be not displeased because of the boy and because of your slave woman. Whatever Sarah says to you, do as she tells you, for through Isaac shall your offspring be named." The covenant line runs through the child of promise, not the child of human scheming — Paul will later build the whole contrast of law and gospel, flesh and promise, on this very household (Galatians 4). And God immediately adds His mercy for the boy being sent away: "And I will make a nation of the son of the slave woman also, because he is your offspring." Letting go, when God commands it, is not abandonment. Abraham can release Ishmael into the hands of a God who has already made promises over him.

**Bread, water, and a God who hears (21:14–21).** Abraham rises early, gives Hagar bread and a skin of water, and sends her away — one of the most painful send-offs in the Bible. She wanders in the wilderness of Beersheba, the water runs out, and she puts the boy under a bush and sits down a bowshot away, "for she said, 'Let me not look on the death of the child.' And as she sat opposite him, she lifted up her voice and wept." Here is a mother at the absolute end — no plan, no water, no strength, nothing but weeping. And then the center of the passage: "And God heard the voice of the boy, and the angel of God called to Hagar from heaven." The boy's name is Ishmael — God hears — and God lives up to the name. "What troubles you, Hagar? Fear not, for God has heard the voice of the boy where he is. Up! Lift up the boy, and hold him fast with your hand, for I will make him into a great nation." Then God opens her eyes, and she sees a well of water — provision that was there before she could see it. She fills the skin, gives the boy a drink, and the narrator closes the account with quiet triumph: "And God was with the boy, and he grew up." He became an expert with the bow and lived in the wilderness of Paran. For every parent who has wept over a child at a distance, feeling helpless to fix it: God hears the voice of the boy where he is — even where you cannot reach. Your tears are not the end of the story, because your child's story is held by the God who hears.

**The covenant at Beersheba (21:22–34).** Abimelech returns, this time with Phicol, the commander of his army, and this time he comes in respect: "God is with you in all that you do." The king who once rebuked Abraham now seeks a covenant with him, having seen that God's hand is on this man. Abraham swears the oath, but he also raises a grievance — a well of water seized by Abimelech's servants. Wells were life in that dry country; a stolen well was no small matter. Abimelech professes ignorance, Abraham presses the matter into the open, and the two make a covenant, sealed with sheep and oxen and with seven ewe lambs set apart as a witness that Abraham dug the well. The place is named Beersheba — the well of seven, the well of the oath. There is honest dealing here worth copying: Abraham does not nurse the grievance in silence or explode over it; he names it plainly at the table of peace, and the covenant is stronger for the truth being told. Then Abraham plants a tamarisk tree at Beersheba and "called there on the name of the LORD, the Everlasting God." The man who once feared there was no fear of God in that region now plants a tree and preaches God's name in it. And the chapter rests: "And Abraham sojourned many days in the land of the Philistines."

### Key verses

> "The LORD visited Sarah as he had said, and the LORD did to Sarah as he had promised." — Genesis 21:1 (ESV)

> "And Sarah said, 'God has made laughter for me; everyone who hears will laugh with me.'" — Genesis 21:6 (ESV)

> "And God heard the voice of the boy, and the angel of God called to Hagar from heaven and said to her, 'What troubles you, Hagar? Fear not, for God has heard the voice of the boy where he is.'" — Genesis 21:17 (ESV)

> "Then God opened her eyes, and she saw a well of water. And she went and filled the skin with water and gave the boy a drink." — Genesis 21:19 (ESV)

> "And Abraham sojourned many days in the land of the Philistines." — Genesis 21:34 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that our shortcuts have consequences that outlive our repentance — a divided household, a mother and boy wandering thirsty in the wilderness, tears under a bush — and no one in this chapter can fix what human scheming broke. But the good news is that God does exactly what He promised at the time He appointed, turns hidden laughter of unbelief into open laughter of joy, hears the voice of the outcast boy where he is, and opens our eyes to provision we could not see — and all of it points to the greater Son of promise, Jesus, born not by human scheming but by God's own power, so that everyone cast out could be brought home.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther loved this chapter's opening, preaching that God's "visiting" of Sarah shows how the Lord keeps His appointments even when every circumstance has made the promise look dead — faith, he taught, clings to the bare word "as he had said." Calvin, on Hagar's expulsion, drew out the hard comfort that God sometimes separates us from what our own flesh produced, yet never abandons those who are sent away, since He hears Ishmael's voice in the desert and makes provision even outside the covenant line.

**Reflection question:** What promise of God has taken so long that you have stopped expecting it — and what would change if you trusted that He will do it "as he had said," at His appointed time?


## Genesis 22 — The Binding of Isaac

### Jesus in this chapter

No chapter in Genesis points to Jesus more clearly, or with more weight, than this one. A father is told to take his son — "your son, your only son Isaac, whom you love" — to the land of Moriah and offer him there. The son carries the wood for his own sacrifice up the mountain. The son asks, "Where is the lamb?" and the father answers, "God will provide for himself the lamb." And God does provide — a ram caught in a thicket, offered in Isaac's place, so the son lives because a substitute dies. Two thousand years later, on that same mountain range in Jerusalem — for Moriah is where the temple would stand (2 Chronicles 3:1) — God the Father gave His Son, His only Son, whom He loves. But there, no voice stopped the knife, and no ram was found in the thicket, because Jesus Himself was the Lamb God provided. Isaac was spared. Jesus was not, so that we would be.

John the Baptist's cry gathers up this whole chapter: "Behold, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!" (John 1:29). And Paul, reflecting on Abraham's offering, reaches for its language to describe God's: "He who did not spare his own Son but gave him up for us all, how will he not also with him graciously give us all things?" (Romans 8:32). The difference between Moriah and Calvary is the difference between the shadow and the substance. Abraham's hand was stayed because a substitute was enough for Isaac. God's hand was not stayed, because nothing less than His own Son was enough for us. Every detail here — the beloved only son, the wood on his back, the third day, the substitute, the name "The LORD will provide" — is a signpost, and all the signposts point one way: to Jesus.

### Commentary

**The test (22:1–2).** "After these things God tested Abraham." The narrator tells us what Abraham does not know: this is a test, not a punishment, and not a God who has changed His mind about the promise. Abraham has the son at last — the laughter, the miracle, the heir through whom the nations will be blessed. Isaac is a young man now, old enough to carry wood and ask hard questions. And into that settled joy comes the most staggering command in the Old Testament: "Take your son, your only son Isaac, whom you love, and go to the land of Moriah, and offer him there as a burnt offering on one of the mountains of which I shall tell you." Every phrase tightens the knot — your son, your only son, Isaac, whom you love. Ishmael is gone; Isaac is the only son of promise left, and God names the father's love out loud, as if to say: I know exactly what I am asking. A burnt offering was total — the whole animal consumed. God is asking Abraham to put the entire promise, the entire future, his entire heart, on the altar, and to trust that the God who gave Isaac against all nature can be trusted even here. Hebrews tells us Abraham's reasoning: he "considered that God was able even to raise him from the dead" (Hebrews 11:19). Abraham did not obey because the command made sense. He obeyed because he had learned who God is — a God who gives life to the dead. Tests like this do not create faith; they reveal and refine it, and they come, as James says, so that proven faith may produce steadfastness.

**The journey to Moriah (22:3–8).** Abraham rose early in the morning — the same early rising we saw when he sent Hagar away, the obedience of a man who does not negotiate with delay. He saddles the donkey, takes two young men and Isaac, and cuts the wood for the burnt offering himself. Then three days of journey. Think about those three days: every step gave Abraham time to turn back, time to argue, time to let the knife in his heart twist. Faith that obeys for three days, with the beloved son walking beside you, is faith at full stretch. On the third day he lifts up his eyes and sees the place from afar. He tells the servants, "Stay here with the donkey; I and the boy will go over there and worship and come again to you." Even his grammar confesses his hope — we will come again, plural. He lays the wood on Isaac's shoulders, and takes in his own hand the fire and the knife, and the two of them walk together. Then Isaac speaks — "My father!" — and Abraham answers, "Here I am, my son," and the boy asks the question that echoes down the centuries: "Behold, the fire and the wood, but where is the lamb for a burnt offering?" Abraham's answer is prophecy whether he fully knows it or not: "God will provide for himself the lamb for a burnt offering, my son." And the narrator adds, with unbearable quietness, "So they went both of them together."

**The altar, the knife, and the voice (22:9–12).** They come to the place of which God had told him, and Abraham builds the altar, lays the wood in order, binds Isaac — Jewish memory calls this whole event the Akedah, the Binding — and lays him on the altar, on top of the wood. Isaac, old enough to carry the wood and strong enough, in all likelihood, to resist an old man, submits. Scripture does not record a struggle. Then Abraham reaches out his hand and takes the knife to slaughter his son. At that exact moment — not before, not during the journey, but at the point of no return — the angel of the LORD calls from heaven: "Abraham, Abraham!" And he said, "Here I am." "Do not lay your hand on the boy or do anything to him, for now I know that you fear God, seeing you have not withheld your son, your only son, from me." God never wanted Isaac's blood; human sacrifice is an abomination He forbids everywhere else in Scripture. He wanted Abraham's unwithheld heart, and He wanted to carve into history a picture no one would ever forget. "Now I know" does not mean God learned something new; it means Abraham's faith has now been shown, proven in the open, to Abraham himself, to Isaac, and to every reader since. Faith that is never tested is faith we only claim. Tested faith is faith we know.

**The ram and the Name (22:13–14).** Abraham lifts up his eyes — the same lifting of eyes as on the third day — and behind him sees a ram, caught in a thicket by his horns. He takes the ram and offers it up as a burnt offering instead of his son. Instead of his son. That is substitution, written into the story of God's people a thousand years before Isaiah would sing of the Servant wounded for our transgressions, and nearly two thousand years before the true Substitute hung on a Roman cross outside Jerusalem. Isaac walks down the mountain alive because something else died in his place. Every believer's story is in that sentence. And Abraham names the place with the name that becomes the chapter's banner: "The LORD Will Provide" — Jehovah-jireh — "as it is said to this day, 'On the mount of the LORD it shall be provided.'" On the mount of the LORD it was provided again, finally and fully, when the Lamb God promised was given on that same mountain country. When we say "the Lord will provide," we are not reciting a slogan about groceries and bills, true as His daily care is. We are confessing Moriah and Calvary: at the place of greatest need, God provides — and what He ultimately provides is Himself.

**The oath sworn again (22:15–19).** The angel of the LORD calls a second time, and now God swears — by Himself, since He has no one greater to swear by (Hebrews 6:13) — "because you have done this and have not withheld your son, your only son, I will surely bless you, and I will surely multiply your offspring as the stars of heaven and as the sand that is on the seashore. And your offspring shall possess the gate of his enemies, and in your offspring shall all the nations of the earth be blessed, because you have obeyed my voice." Notice the grace of the order: the promise was pure gift long before this test; God does not now pay Abraham wages. Rather, the obedience that flowed from faith is taken up into God's own oath as the occasion for confirming, publicly and irrevocably, what grace had already given. James uses this moment to show that Abraham was "justified by works" in the sense that his faith was completed and proven by his action (James 2:21–23) — the same Abraham Paul uses to prove justification by faith alone. There is no contradiction: the faith that justifies is never alone; it climbs mountains. Then Abraham returns to his young men, and they go together to Beersheba. Isaac is there in the "they," though the text lets the father carry the weight of the ending. A short genealogy closes the chapter (22:20–24): news arrives that Milcah has borne children to Abraham's brother Nahor — among them Bethuel, who will father Rebekah. Even as the knife is put away, God is already preparing the bride for the son who was spared.

### Key verses

> "He said, 'Take your son, your only son Isaac, whom you love, and go to the land of Moriah, and offer him there as a burnt offering on one of the mountains of which I shall tell you.'" — Genesis 22:2 (ESV)

> "Abraham said, 'God will provide for himself the lamb for a burnt offering, my son.' So they went both of them together." — Genesis 22:8 (ESV)

> "But the angel of the LORD called to him from heaven and said, 'Abraham, Abraham!' And he said, 'Here I am.' He said, 'Do not lay your hand on the boy or do anything to him, for now I know that you fear God, seeing you have not withheld your son, your only son, from me.'" — Genesis 22:11–12 (ESV)

> "And Abraham lifted up his eyes and looked, and behold, behind him was a ram, caught in a thicket by his horns. And Abraham went and took the ram and offered it up as a burnt offering instead of his son." — Genesis 22:13 (ESV)

> "So Abraham called the name of that place, 'The LORD will provide'; as it is said to this day, 'On the mount of the LORD it shall be provided.'" — Genesis 22:14 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that sin truly requires a death — the knife over Isaac tells the truth about what our rebellion deserves, and no amount of religion or effort can put that knife down, because the Judge who commands holiness cannot simply pretend the debt is not owed. But the good news is Moriah's own name: the LORD will provide — and He did, first in a ram caught in a thicket so a beloved son could walk down the mountain alive, and finally in His own beloved Son, the Lamb God provided for Himself, who was not spared so that every Isaac among us, every guilty son and daughter under judgment, could be spared forever.

**A note from the Reformers.** Luther preached on this chapter with trembling, calling Abraham's obedience a faith that wrestled against every appearance — against nature, against reason, against a father's heart — and clung to the promise alone, concluding that Abraham believed God could raise the dead rather than let His word fall. Calvin likewise stressed that Abraham's trial was not God tempting him to evil but God proving and displaying a faith God Himself had given, so that the church in every age would have a pattern of trusting God's promise when His command seems to contradict it.

**Reflection question:** What is the thing you love most that you are still withholding from God — and what would it mean to lay it on the altar, trusting the God who provides and who did not spare His own Son for you?

## Genesis 23 — The Death of Sarah

### Jesus in this chapter

Jesus is present in this chapter the way a sunrise is present in the first gray light before it: not yet visible, but everything here is waiting for Him. Sarah dies in a land that was promised to her family but that she never owned — except for one field with a cave in it, bought as a burial place. Abraham and Sarah lived and died, as Hebrews says, "in faith, not having received the things promised, but having seen them and greeted them from afar" (Hebrews 11:13). Their only real estate in Canaan was a grave. That grave is a sermon: God's people can bury their dead in hope, in ground staked to a promise, because death is not the last word over those who belong to the covenant. That hope only makes sense because of Jesus, who would one day lie in a borrowed tomb in this same land — and walk out of it, the firstfruits of all who sleep in Him.

Christ is also here by contrast and by promise in the way Abraham speaks of himself: "I am a sojourner and foreigner among you" (23:4). Abraham, the friend of God and heir of the world, owns nothing and confesses it. Jesus would later say the Son of Man had nowhere to lay His head, and He too was buried in a tomb that was not His own. But where Abraham bought a grave for his household's hope, Jesus emptied His grave for the whole world's hope. Machpelah says: we are waiting. The empty tomb says: the wait is over.

### Commentary

**Sarah dies at Kiryat Arba (23:1–2).** "Sarah lived 127 years; these were the years of the life of Sarah. And Sarah died at Kiriath Arba (that is, Hebron) in the land of Canaan." Sarah is the only woman in Scripture whose age at death is recorded — a quiet honor for the mother of the promise, the woman who laughed and then laughed with joy, who followed her husband from Ur to a land she never possessed. Isaac is about thirty-seven. Abraham's great companion of a hundred years of sojourning is gone. And the text says Abraham "went in to mourn for Sarah and to weep for her." The friend of God weeps. Faith does not cancel grief; it gives grief a place to stand. Abraham does not weep as those who have no hope — but he does weep, openly, and the Bible records his tears without embarrassment. That matters for every grieving reader: the man who trusted God most in Genesis is shown on the ground beside his dead wife, mourning. Tears and trust are not enemies. Jesus Himself would weep at a friend's grave, even knowing resurrection was minutes away. Abraham mourns, and then — as grief must, in its season — he rises from before his dead and acts. There are things love must do even with a broken heart: a grave must be found, and the dead must be buried with honor.

**"I am a sojourner and foreigner among you" (23:3–6).** Abraham goes to the Hittites, the sons of Heth, who hold the land around Hebron, and he makes his request with characteristic humility: "I am a sojourner and foreigner among you. Give me property among you for a burying place, that I may bury my dead out of my sight." Sit with the strangeness of that. This is the man to whom God said, "To your offspring I will give this land." All of it. And he is asking to buy a burial plot, as a foreigner, from the people living on his own promised inheritance. Faith often looks exactly like that: holding a promise that covers a whole country while owning nothing but the plot you are negotiating for. The Hittites answer with great respect — "Hear us, my lord; you are a prince of God among us. Bury your dead in the choicest of our tombs. None of us will withhold from you his tomb to hinder you from burying your dead." They offer the free use of their tombs. It is generous, and Abraham will not take it. A borrowed tomb in another man's field would leave Sarah — and the promise she carried — resting on Hittite charity. Abraham wants ground that is his family's own, bought and witnessed, a stake in the land God promised. Hope, in Abraham, is not vague. It purchases property.

**The negotiation at the gate (23:7–16).** What follows is one of the most detailed business transactions in the Bible, and the detail is the point. Abraham bows to the people of the land and asks them to intercede with Ephron the Hittite for the cave of Machpelah, at the end of his field: "For the full price let him give it to me in your presence as property for a burying place." Ephron, sitting among his people at the city gate — the courthouse and marketplace of the ancient world — answers with the elaborate courtesy of Near Eastern bargaining: "No, my lord, hear me: I give you the field, and I give you the cave that is in it... bury your dead." It sounds like a gift; it is an opening bid. Abraham bows again and insists on paying the full price of the field. Then Ephron names it, with studied casualness: "My lord, listen to me: a piece of land worth four hundred shekels of silver, what is that between you and me? Bury your dead." Four hundred shekels was no small sum — a very large price, roughly ten times what Jeremiah would later pay for a field, and Abraham does not haggle. "Abraham weighed out to Ephron... four hundred shekels of silver, according to the weights current among the merchants." Weighed, in public, at the gate, before witnesses. Why does Scripture slow down for all this? Because this purchase is a legal landmark: the first piece of the promised land to actually become Abraham's property, deeded and witnessed, is a tomb. God's promise-keeping is not a fog of feelings; it has weights, measures, witnesses, and a deed. And there is a word here for people rebuilding a life: Abraham pays full price and does it in the open. No shortcuts, no side deals, no buried fine print. The man of faith does honest business at the gate, where everyone can see.

**The field is made over — and Sarah is buried (23:17–20).** The deed is spelled out in full legal form: "So the field of Ephron in Machpelah, which was to the east of Mamre, the field with the cave that was in it and all the trees that were in the field, throughout its whole area, was made over to Abraham as a possession in the presence of the Hittites, before all who went in at the gate of his city." Field, cave, every tree, the whole area — nothing left vague, nothing left to dispute. Then the chapter lands where it was always going: "After this, Abraham buried Sarah his wife in the cave of the field of Machpelah east of Mamre (that is, Hebron) in the land of Canaan." The field and cave "were made over to Abraham as property for a burying place." That cave will receive Abraham himself (25:9–10), then Isaac, Rebekah, Leah, and finally Jacob, carried home from Egypt to lie with his fathers (49:29–32; 50:13). Generation after generation of the promise family will be laid in that one purchased cave, each burial a fresh confession: we die in this land because we believe we will live in it — that God who promised it will keep His word, even on the far side of death. Hebron's grave is not despair. It is a deposit. When Jesus rose from the dead, He proved that every body laid in Machpelah in faith was laid there in a hope that will not be disappointed.

### Key verses

> "And Sarah died at Kiriath Arba (that is, Hebron) in the land of Canaan, and Abraham went in to mourn for Sarah and to weep for her." — Genesis 23:2 (ESV)

> "I am a sojourner and foreigner among you. Give me property among you for a burying place, that I may bury my dead out of my sight." — Genesis 23:4 (ESV)

> "So the field of Ephron in Machpelah, which was to the east of Mamre, the field with the cave that was in it and all the trees that were in the field, throughout its whole area, was made over to Abraham as a possession in the presence of the Hittites, before all who went in at the gate of his city." — Genesis 23:17 (ESV)

> "After this, Abraham buried Sarah his wife in the cave of the field of Machpelah east of Mamre (that is, Hebron) in the land of Canaan." — Genesis 23:19 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that death comes even to the mother of the promise — that a hundred years of faith still end at a graveside, that the heir of the whole land owns nothing in it but a tomb, and that we too will stand where Abraham stood, weeping over someone we love in a world that is not yet what God promised it will be. But the good news is that Abraham bought that grave in faith, not despair: the God who keeps promises could be trusted on the far side of death, and He proved it forever when Jesus — buried in another man's tomb in that same land — rose again, so that every grave of every believer is now, like Machpelah, only a purchased resting place on land that will one day be fully ours.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin remarked on the care Scripture takes with this purchase, teaching that Abraham's insistence on a full, public price shows faith laying hold of the promise in concrete form — Abraham wanted the land's first owned portion to be a burial place precisely because he believed his family would possess the land beyond death. The Reformers also noted Abraham's tears as a standing rebuke to any cold pretense that faith forbids grief: the patriarch wept, and then, having wept, rose and acted in hope.

**Reflection question:** Where in your life are you being asked to stake a claim on God's promise — to act in faith and hope — even though, like Abraham, all you can see right now is loss?


## Genesis 24 — A Bride for Isaac

### Jesus in this chapter

This is the longest chapter in Genesis, and it is, at heart, a love story God is telling on purpose. A father sends his trusted servant on a long journey to find a bride for his beloved son — a son who, in the last great chapter, was offered on Moriah and received back as if from the dead. The servant goes with gifts, prays for guidance, and brings the bride home, and the son waits in the field at evening and receives her, and loves her. It does not take much imagination to hear the greater story underneath, and the church has heard it for centuries — gently, not forcing every detail, but hearing it. God the Father, in His time, sends for a bride for His Son. The bride is not worthy in herself; she is chosen, called, and brought home by grace. And Jesus Himself would tell of a Father preparing a wedding feast for His Son, and Paul would say that marriage itself points to Christ and His church, and Revelation ends with a bride made ready for the Lamb.

Isaac, the child of promise, is a quiet figure here — he does not go and get his bride; she is brought to him. That passivity is part of the pointer. Our salvation does not rest on our searching for God and finally finding Him. It rests on God seeking us, calling us by name at a well we thought was ordinary, and bringing us home to a Son who receives us and loves us. Rebekah's simple answer — "I will go" — is the answer of faith every believer eventually gives to the call of God. Nobody drags her. Grace makes her willing.

### Commentary

**Abraham's charge to his servant (24:1–9).** Abraham is now old, advanced in years, and the LORD has blessed him in all things. Sarah is buried. Isaac is grown and unmarried, and for Abraham this is not a small family worry — the whole promise runs through Isaac's line. So Abraham calls the oldest servant of his house, the man who rules over all that he has, and puts him under a solemn oath with his hand under Abraham's thigh: you shall not take a wife for my son from the daughters of the Canaanites among whom I dwell, but you shall go to my country and to my kindred. The line of promise must not be folded into the worship and ways of Canaan. This is not snobbery about blood; it is a father guarding the faith of the next generation. The servant raises the honest difficulty: what if the woman will not follow me? Shall I take your son back to the land you came from? Abraham is absolute: "See to it that you do not take my son back there." Under no circumstances does the promise go backward. The God who brought Abraham out of that land will send His angel before the servant. And if the woman will not come, the servant is free of the oath — but Isaac does not go back. There is a word here for anyone in recovery and anyone rebuilding a family: some doors God has closed stay closed. We do not go back to the old country to get what God has promised to provide on the road forward.

**The prayer at the well (24:10–14).** The servant takes ten camels — loaded with good things from his master, a traveling picture of the father's generosity — and journeys to Mesopotamia, to the city of Nahor. He arrives at evening, at the time when women go out to draw water, and makes the camels kneel beside the well outside the city. Then he prays, and his prayer is worth studying for its plainness. He does not demand a sign from the sky. He asks God to work through an ordinary act of kindness: "O LORD, God of my master Abraham, please grant me success today and show steadfast love to my master Abraham." Here the chapter's great word appears — *hesed*, the steadfast, covenant love of God that does not let go. The servant is asking God to be to Abraham what God has always been: loyal in love, faithful to His promise. And the test he proposes is a test of character: the young woman who not only gives him a drink but offers to water his camels too — ten camels, each drinking many gallons after a desert journey — that is the woman of generous, working kindness fit to join this household. He is not looking for beauty first. He is looking for a heart that serves without being asked twice.

**Rebekah comes to the well (24:15–28).** Before he had finished speaking, Rebekah comes out with her jar on her shoulder. Scripture often works that way — the answer is already on the road while the prayer is still on our lips. She is beautiful, she is of Abraham's kindred — Bethuel's daughter, Milcah's granddaughter, of the very family the servant was sent to — and she does exactly, and more than, what he prayed: "Drink, my lord," and she lowers her jar quickly; then, "I will draw water for your camels also, until they have finished drinking." She runs, she empties, she draws again and again. The man gazes at her in silence, "to learn whether the LORD had prospered his journey or not" — wise restraint; he watches before he concludes. Then he brings out the gifts, a gold ring and bracelets, and asks the two questions that matter: whose daughter are you, and is there room in your father's house for us to spend the night? Her answer names the exact household he sought, and adds, with the same generosity, that there is straw and fodder and room to lodge. The servant bows his head and worships the LORD on the spot: God has not forsaken His *hesed* and His faithfulness toward Abraham. Worship, in this chapter, happens at a well, in the middle of an errand, because a servant recognized God's hand in an ordinary afternoon. That is where most of us will meet God's guidance too — not in thunder, but in the middle of the work, as He leads us step by step in the way.

**"Do not delay me" — and Rebekah decides (24:29–61).** Rebekah runs home, and her brother Laban — who notices the gold, as Laban will always notice the gold — runs out to invite the servant in with unusual warmth: "Come in, O blessed of the LORD." The camels are fed, feet are washed, food is set out — and the servant will not eat until he has told his errand. There is a holy urgency in a man who puts God's business before his own supper. He retells the whole story: the oath, the prayer, the well, the exact answer. Retelling God's guidance is not wasted breath; it is how other people get to weigh whether the LORD has spoken. Then he puts the question plainly to Bethuel and Laban: if you will show steadfast love and faithfulness to my master, tell me; and if not, tell me. Their answer is one of the clearest confessions of providence in Genesis: "The thing has come from the LORD; we cannot speak to you bad or good. Behold, Rebekah is before you; take her and go." The servant bows to the earth before the LORD, brings out gold, silver, and garments for Rebekah and rich gifts for her family. The next morning, when the family asks for ten days' delay — a natural, human request, a last stretch of goodbye — the servant answers, "Do not delay me, since the LORD has prospered my way." And so they put the decision where it belongs: they call Rebekah herself. "Will you go with this man?" She said, "I will go." No long speech. A young woman leaves her father's house, her country, everything familiar, on the word of a story she has only just heard, because she believes the thing has come from the LORD. They bless her — "Our sister, may you become thousands of ten thousands" — and she rides away with her nurse, following the servant toward a husband she has never seen. Faith often looks just like that: a blessing at the door, a long road, and a simple "I will go."

**Isaac in the field at evening (24:62–67).** The camera, so to speak, turns home. Isaac has come from Beer-lahai-roi and is living in the Negeb, and he goes out to meditate in the field toward evening. He is a man still carrying grief — his mother is dead, and the text will say plainly in a moment that he was comforted after her death only now. He lifts up his eyes and sees camels coming. Rebekah lifts up her eyes, sees Isaac, dismounts, and asks the servant, "Who is that man, walking in the field to meet us?" "It is my master." She takes her veil and covers herself. The servant tells Isaac all the things he had done — the whole long story of prayer and providence — and then the chapter closes with quiet, deep satisfaction: "Then Isaac brought her into the tent of Sarah his mother and took Rebekah, and she became his wife, and he loved her. So Isaac was comforted after his mother's death." The bride enters the dead mother's tent; grief and joy live in the same sentence, as they so often do in real life. God did not erase Isaac's sorrow by pretending it was nothing. He comforted it by giving love again. That is how the LORD meets people where they are — not always by removing the empty chair, but by filling the house with new mercy.

### Key verses

> "O LORD, God of my master Abraham, please grant me success today and show steadfast love to my master Abraham." — Genesis 24:12 (ESV)

> "Blessed be the LORD, the God of my master Abraham, who has not forsaken his steadfast love and his faithfulness toward my master. As for me, the LORD has led me in the way to the house of my master's kinsmen." — Genesis 24:27 (ESV)

> "Then Laban and Bethuel answered and said, 'The thing has come from the LORD; we cannot speak to you bad or good. Behold, Rebekah is before you; take her and go, that she may be the wife of your master's son, as the LORD has spoken.'" — Genesis 24:50–51 (ESV)

> "And they called Rebekah and said to her, 'Will you go with this man?' She said, 'I will go.'" — Genesis 24:58 (ESV)

> "Then Isaac brought her into the tent of Sarah his mother and took Rebekah, and she became his wife, and he loved her. So Isaac was comforted after his mother's death." — Genesis 24:67 (ESV)

### The bad news and the good news

The bad news is that left to ourselves we would settle — settle for Canaan's ways, settle for going back to the old country, settle for a life shaped by whatever is nearest instead of by God's promise — and many a family line has been lost exactly there, one reasonable compromise at a time. But the good news is that God does not forsake His steadfast love: He goes before His servants on roads they have never walked, answers prayer at ordinary wells, makes a willing heart say "I will go," and brings the bride home to the beloved Son — and that is still His way, for He is bringing a people from every nation home to Jesus, not because they were the finest, but because His *hesed* would not let them go.

**A note from the Reformers.** Calvin, on the servant's prayer, praised its modesty: the man asks God to guide him through ordinary means and a woman's character rather than demanding wonders, and Calvin took this as a pattern for seeking God's will — pray plainly, watch providence carefully, and worship when the way becomes clear. Luther delighted in Rebekah's "I will go" as a picture of faith's readiness: she hears the word, believes the promise attached to it, and leaves what she knows for a husband she has not yet seen.

**Reflection question:** When God puts His call in front of you — plain, unexpected, and asking you to leave something familiar — is your answer closer to "give me ten days" or to Rebekah's "I will go," and what would help you answer today?

## Genesis 25 — Abraham's Death and the Twins

### Jesus in this chapter

This chapter closes Abraham's story and opens the next act of the promise — and Jesus stands at both ends of it. At the close of Abraham's life we are meant to look back and see that everything God promised, He performed: a son, a line, a future. Jesus would say, "Abraham rejoiced that he would see my day. He saw it and was glad" (John 8:56). Abraham died looking forward, by faith, to a day he never lived to see — the day of Christ. His grave at Machpelah is not a monument to a finished story but a marker on a road that leads to Jesus, and every believer who dies in faith dies the same way Abraham did: gathered to his people, full of years, still expecting God to keep the rest of His word.

At the chapter's other end, in Rebekah's womb, two boys struggle — and God's word over them announces the pattern that will run all the way to the cross: "the older shall serve the younger." God does not choose the way the world chooses. He passes over the strong firstborn and takes the younger, the weaker, the unlikely — not because Jacob is better, for this chapter already shows Jacob grasping, but because grace is free. Paul builds Romans 9 on this very oracle, before the boys had done anything good or bad, "in order that God's purpose of election might continue, not because of works but because of him who calls." The line to Jesus runs through Jacob — Israel — by sheer grace. The Savior of sinners comes from a family saved by grace, for sinners saved by grace. That is the only kind of family there is.

### Commentary
